COUNTY OF CHESTER. WILD BIRDS PROTECTION ACTS, 1880, 1894, AND 1896.

"VOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the following is _ll a copy of an ORDER which has been made by the Right Honourable Sir Matthew White Ridley, Baronet, M.P., Secretary of State for the Home Department, viz : WILD BIRDS (ENGLAND). The Wild Birds Protection (County of Chester) Order, 1898, Dated 15th F bruary, 1898. In pursuance of the powers couferred upon me by the Wild Birds Protection , Acts, 1880 -to 1896, and upon application by the County Council of Chester, I hereby make the following Order,which shall apply to the whole of the County of Chester :- 1. This Order may be cited as " The. Wild Birds Pro- tection (County of ()nester) Order, 1898." 2. The time during which the killing and taking of wild birds, or any of them, is prohibited by the Wild Birth Protection Act, 1880 (43 and 44 Vic., cap. 35),5ha1l be extended in the County of Chester) so as to be from the Ist day of March to the 12th day of August in each year. 3. The Act of 1880. as extended by Section 2of this Order, shall apply within the whole of the County of Chester to the Kestrel and Heron as if those species were included in the Schedule of that Act. 4. The taking or klliug of the following species of birds, namely :—Kingfisher, Great; Crested Grebe,Heron, Goldfinch and Owl, shall he prohibited within the whole of the County of Chester during the whole of that period of the year to which the protection of wild birds wider the Act of 1880 (as extended by Section 2 of this Order) does not extend. . 5. The taking or aestroving of the Eggs of the Owl, the Biitern, tbelKingfisher, the Kestrel. the Heron, the Sheldrake, the Dunliti,the Blackheaded Gull,the Common Tern, and the Oyster Catcher, shill be prohibited in the whole of the County of Chester. And I make the following further Order, which shall apply within the portion of the Hundred of Wirral coloured pink on the map; deposited in the Home Oft, and in the office of the County Council, and embrac- ing thelow-lying lands and sandhills situate between the estuaries of the Rivers Dee and Mersey, of which the boundaries shall commence to the south at a point of the main highway of the RESTRICTION ON MOYEMENT OF ANIMALS OUT OF OR INFO PLACE OF OUTBREAK.

(2) Any horse, as,or mule which is not diseased may be moved thereout to any place.

(3) All other animals whic are in such shed, stable, buileling,field, or other place,and which are not disoasee, may, and should in every ease where it is practieable,be moved thereout by the owner, un,er the supervision of an Inspector or other officer of the Local Authority, to some convenient place of detention for the purpose of isolation, and the animals so moved shall, after their arrival at the place of detention, be there kept separate by the owner thereof from all other animals and not moved thereout for a period of seven days. (4,) Provided that any animal which is not diseased may be moved out of such shed,etable, building,field, or other place as afore.said, or from the said place of deten- tion, to the nearest available slaughter-house under the supervision of an Inspector or other officer of the Local Authority for tte purpose of being forthwith slaugh- tered.

6.—lt shall not be lawful for any person to remove any dung, litter, broken fodder, or any other thing that has been in contact with or used for or about any diseased aninaal, out of any shed, stable, building, fitld, or other place in which a diseaced animal is, or has died, or been slaup,htercd, or has been kcpt at the date of such death or slaughter,except to a place approved by an Inspector of tha Local Authority, and to which animals will not have accees, and any such dung or other thing aforesaid so removed shall be forthwith burnt or otherwise des- troyed, or shall ba forthwith disinfected and, where practicable, buried to the satisfaction of the Inspector.

DIGGING UP.

B.—lt shall not, be lawful for any person, except with the License of the Board, or permission in writing of an Inspector of the Board, to dig up, or cause to be dug up, the carcase of any animal that has been tmriee, whether under this Order or otherwise. OCCUPIERS TO GIVE FACILITIES FOR CLEANSING.

10.—Where the power of causing any ptaee, thing, or vebicle to ba cleansed and disin ectsd under this Order is exercised by a Local Authority, the owner and occu• pier and person in charge of the place, thing, or vehicl,e, shall give all reasonable facili icis for that purpose, and any person failing to give such facilities shall be deemed guilty of an offenco against the Act of 1894. PROUIBITION OF EXPOSURE OR MOVEMENT OF DISEASED OR SUSPECTED ANIMALS. 11.—(1) It shall rot be lawful for any persan— (a) To expose a diseased or suspected animal in a market or fair, or in a sale yard. or other public or private place where animals are commonly ex- posed for sale ; or (b) To place a dise,sed, or suspectei, animal in a lair or other place adjacent to or connected with a market or a fair, or where animals are com- monly placed before exposure for sale ; or (o) To send or carry, or cause to be sent or carried, direased, or suspected, animal on a railway, canal, river, or inland navigation,or in a coasting vessel ; or (d) To carry, lead, or drive, or cause to be carried, led, or driven, a diseased, or suspected, animal on a highway or thoroughfare ; or (e) To place or keep a diseased,or susneeted,animal on common or uninclosed land, or in a field or place insufficiently fenced, or in a field adjoining a highway unless that field is so fenced or situate that animals therein cermet in any manner come in contact with animals passing along that high- way or crazing on the aides thereof ; or (f) To graze a disclosed, or suspected, animal on pasture being on the sides of a highway or ; (g) To allow a disease,l, or suspected, animal to stray on a highway or thoroughfare or on the sides thereof, or on common or uninclosed land, or in a field or place insufficiently fenced. 2.—Notwithstanding anything in this Order an animal exposed or otherwiee dealt with inTeontravention of this Article may be moved by or under the direction of an Inspector of the Local Authority to some convenient and isolated place. MOVEMENT OF ANIMALS, &c., WITH LICENSE

EXTENSION OF CERTAIN SECTIONS OF DISEASES OF ANIMALS AC V, 1894. 16.—Horses, aoies, and mules (as well as the animals specified in the Act of 1894) shall be animals, and anth- rax (that is to say, the disease called or known as anth- rax, splenic fever, or splenic apoplexy of animals) shall he a disease, for the purposes of the following sections of the Act of 1894 (namely) SPction forty-threo (powers of p,)lice) ; Section forty-four (pourers of inspectors) and also for the purposes of all other eections of the said Act containing provisiJns relative to or consequent en the provisions of those sections and this Order, including such sections as relate to offences and legal prcceed- lugs. PRESUMPTION OF KNOWLEDGE OF DISEASE.

17.—Where the own-e-r-o-r-p-crion in charge of an I animal is charged with an offence against the Ac %of 1894 relative to anthrax, he shall be presumed to have known of the existence of that disease, unless and until he shows, to the satisfaction of the Court, that he had not knowledge thereof and could not with reasonable diligence have obtained that knowjedge. OFFENCES. 18.—(1) If any animal or any thing is moved in con- travention of this Order, the owner of the animal or thing,and the person for the time being in charge thereof. and the person cawing, directine, or permitting the movement, and the person moving or conveying the animal or thing, and the owner a ad the charterer and the master of the vessel in which it is moved, and the eontignee or other person receiving or keeping it know- ing it to have been moved in contravention as aforesaid, and the occupier of the place from which the animal or thing is moved, shall,each according to and in respect of his own acts and defanits,be deemed guilty of an offence against the Act of 1894. (2) If a carcase is removed or buried or destroyed or otherwise dealt with in contraverition of this Order, the owner of the carcase, and the person for the time being in charge thereof, and the person causing, directing. or permitting the carcase to be so removed, buried, des- troyed, or otherwise dealt with, and the consignee or lother person receiving or keeping it knowing it to have been removed in contravention as aforesaid, shall, each according to and in respect of his own acts and defaults be deemed guilty of an offence againAt thn Act of 1894. (3) If a person, with a view to unlawfully evade or defeat the eperation of this Order, allows an animal to stray, he sh iti he dtemed guilty of an cffence ageinst tbe Act of 1894.

Its Purity.

A WARIUNGTONIAN IN CItiCCIETH.

HIS VIEWS OF MEN AND THINGS. Well, the Yule-tide is over, gni it is a Yule-ide hereabouts, if logs of wood have anything to do with that season. The lieges have now turned ound again from their eating and drinking, ani he sacred cloee of the season finds them looking fiercely into one antler's eyes, and the Church versus Dissent is again the question. They are gasl,,.g fail of principle and the other thing. Well, as Artemus Ward used to say, " Let them gush I" The rough weather, just now, is enough to knock all the gushing out of anybody, and even the dog " Trap " has g(ot a wheezing and a sneezing, wi ich don't seem com- fortable to him. I don's think i's the influenza, but its a good imitation. I got all mixci with that "Tradition of the Ca4le '' the other week. " Oh 1 it was an 'orrible tale." My informant tells me, al- though he admits the main features of the story, especially the tragic parts. have foundation in fact. Surely that's enough tor Yule-tide. So the thou- sands that come next year to this now celebrated old pile, and may al,o see the raging main of Cardigan Bay" enjoying itself over the covered fair domains .of the two rival chiefs with the jaw-breaking names, the sad waters teach- ing a moral to naughty tersons of all time. I have just been into "town," for I live a few stone- throws from its streets and its beauties. I have made several sincere vows to see that notorious 'Black-rock," and its black cave, and its safe bath- ing sands, which sands are said to be so fine, as to take the shine out of Southpo; t,—but every attempt has been futile, and I have no more notion of that black cave than I have of the Black Hole of Cal- cutta ! However the time will come, and in the meantime lam delighted by the wo'ntt-be-denied foaming waters, that burst against that Castle rock, as if pitchiug into it in anger, vide the chief- tain outrage ; and the advancing foes of the same waters rushing over the nice neat esplanade and Marine Terrace. Eending up their spray humorously over pedestrians, and making bard struggles to reach the dwellings, where windowe-full of the fair sex are admiring the attempts ; and then amidst it all the eternal roar of the sea, in full-toned chorus, load, and sweet, and ever-tuneful," Roll on thou deep and dark blue ocean, roll, I"—its just as well that Byron should be quoted sometimes,—l mean, of course. his lordship of that name. When "the rolling sea "thus chases one away, with the chorus still In the ear, there is ever the sweet shops and pleasant people to criti- cise. This little town, although originally only— and to some extent still—a fishing village, bears all the orthodox displays of a watering place, and nobody need complain that the comforts of visitors are neglected. They boast of their archi- tecture and architects here, of course, and i well they may, for all s very credit- able. There are at least two of every trade in the town, but in hotels and public-houses there are far more, which may not be an anomaly considering that visitors are not, all teetotalers. The na.tivee I find out, are a sober lot, notwithstanding the young one I recorded as not hieing sober, and , the tradespeople are very pleasant and quite oblig- ing. The pluralit‘ of businesses gives stimulus to the suave deportment o: all, but this I suppose is natural. and as Sam Slick says, "You cannot alter human nature." I imagine that there are excep- tions to this dual state of professionalism, for I know only of one solicitor, one policeman, one bar- ber, and no sweep ! There may be necessity jor more some day. The Chri4mas celebration has been avery quiet one all along the line, but the ewYear's Day was rather more lively, for, al- though no midnight well-wishers arrived, by early dawn groups of two or three urchins assailed our ears in very understandeble English by announc- ing that the New Year had arrived, and 1809 cer- tainly was memorialized by any quantity of suc- ceeding groups persistently crying "Happy New Year, Sir," imagining that the hortnitage was solely occupied by one. Ihe success on of .luese wishers became so overpowering, itwo require a bank to satisfy all: They had ihe place 11 to them- selves and the bombardment was kept uP by further groups of smiling boys who were qualitled to max a sixpence out of a miser. I have not seen or heard of any singing or service about here, during the festive seasons. I must acknowledge the heaps of beautiful "season cards " which we have received, and it is plea. ant to know that you can live in a hermitage and not be torgotton. Well, 1 will borrow the smiling boys motto, and say "A Happy New Year, to you, sir." 'There never were such times for cards and c4her works of art, and I am sure that be- nighted Britaie, if it does not exactly know this neighbourhood it has by this: time received suffi- cient pictures of it. Bovril is a triumph in scientific demonstration. In the smallest possible bulk it has every virtue an article of food can possess—it is nourishing and strengthening —it is a stimulant and a warmth-giver, and besides be- ing eminently appetising, is assimilable by persons of the weakest digestive powers.

carrying an excessive number of Christmas and Few Years billet doux, to charm the hearts of those ivho might be depressed. It is wondeiful what a little thing brings delight and comfort to some people, as I found out when I gave a trifle to an old chap, who was breaking stones on the highway, who appeared for hours to keep his eyes and his arms on one job, as he pegged away at these hard-hearted stones, to smash them into useful sizes. The compliments of the s3ason, I said to him, and he suddenly raised his head in surprise. That head doesn't often rise, it never has risen very high. Who he was I don't know,hut he Woke I as if he would be the better for a Government Act to pension the aged. Mild, even sati:fied, he looked, and like the Salisbury Market nags "purged of all earthly passions," was quite submissive to his fate. He thought I was a mission- ary! Well, no, I said, not a paid one at any rate, although I have been one of a sort for at least twenty years ; that is, I discourage cursing and swearing. Then again for the same space of time, I have employed my boot toes in kicking orange peel off pavements to the dilapidation of the soles. A medical friend of mine said this latter philanthropy was hurtful to his profession, and in the coolest, bloodthirsty manner, threatened to buy six-pennyworth of oranges weekly and distribute the peel on places where it could nicely break arms and legs. But I didn't believe him; it was only his play' ulness. The festive season about here is kept mostly outside of dwellings. Hunting the hare is in rampant force; shooting partridg:s in high feather, and as for fishing, well I don't know about thst ' • but this I know that at easy distances there are plenty of streams, lakes,ancl bays to delight the souls of tha disciples of Izaak Walton. And out- side too, are the birds in great number singing and , chirping gaily, occasionally ; the dear little robin visits us, and in the lanes we have the black-bird, goldfin.ches, bullfinches and others, while we have those other feathery fellows they call Jack-daws, who fill the chimneys with nests if you let them, and at this present moment there is a magpie sitt- ing on a senall tree in front of the window where I am writing. Ah ! he sees me looking at him.and like the modest bird as he is, he has flown away, to visit somebody who won't look at him—but they must mind their spoons !

For Influenza.

Now that Influenza is making havoc everywhere a few words about the preventive qualities of Bovril will be both welcome and seasonable. From an occasional cup of Bovril (especially when the brain and body are fatigued by protracted work or any other cause) we derive a certain amount of nourishment, stimulation and warmth : without these the system is ill-equipped to ward off an attack of influenza. Bovril is a mainstay and a sustenant, so stimulating and re-invigorating in its effects as to act as a most effectual bodyguard against the dangers of germ diseases, colds, chills and other ailments common to the winter season. Bovril not only wards off disease; it makes blood, bone, muscle and brain, serves as fuel to keep up the fire of vitality; and, as a true food, effects permanent good.

LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAR NELLIE, One of the prettiest toques which I have seem this season, was one of sequin net, with folds of black tulle laid lightly round the crown. On the left side were clusters of red roses and foliage, with birds of a lighter shade intermixed, and standing up above them was a choice black ostrich tip. . . .

-As for spring fashions now spring is getting so near, there is beginning to be abundabces of them o be seen. The best I have seen, though, is a stylish coat of cloth, with stitchings of silk facings of white cloth, and pearl buttons. Steel or silver buttons look well on this model, which is very effective in light cloth, stitched white, and with purl or steel buttons.

Tailor-made gown reqiiire very little additional trimming, but the iitut core and exl.ctitude is necessary in making up, or it may be utterly ruined. Most of the lawn, antelope, and pater grey tints are stitched with white, but if a deeper shade than the material is us+-.AI for stitching, it must not be-too dark a contras+, or it gives a common effect. On black and very dArk material', pe.:e-coluured stitchings, notably those in amber, pale blue, heliotrope, . and biscuit, are exceedingly effective, especially if the revers are faced with silk of the same tint torraini4 a foundation under guipure or sequined net. One with grace- fully pointed tunic is already in favour for very early spring gowns. Tnis costume is of light fa.wn•coloured cloth, stitched with 'pale cinnsmon-brown, and the coat bodice has revers of ivory satin and a border of narrow skunk fur. The fitted lining is in front, filled in with rucked ivory surah to form a vest, and the fronts of cloth lie over this vest, and nearly meet at the waist. There is a high collar, wilich does not come round to the front, and leaves ample opening for a big jabot of lace. The Lurline costhme is a dainty little gown suited to a tall, slim, and rather long-waisted figure. It should be made of faced cloth in a very pate shade of grey, as this is the colour which best befl:s a slim, figure. A narrow, machine•stitched bias fold of satin outltnes the coat and the frill which forms both the back and the jabot on either side of the silk vest. The skirt forms a tunic or double skirt, also outlined by a blnd of satin. The collar of the coat- bodice is a very original one, and a fall bow of lace fast,ens the neck.

A very taking coat and skirt for early, spring wear is one made in pale tan face cloth ; the little coat fastens invisibly. the front bssque falling in a rounded tab, braiding appearing on the collar, revers, and front cf the coat, carried out in black tubular and narrow braid. The long skirt is alose- fitting over the hips and wide at the feet; it is trimmed all round and down the fronts with braiding to mateh, and the coat is lined with pale blue glace silk. Another charming dress is of pale grey faced cloth, braided in a scalloped design with grey and silver braid. The skirt is cut in two pieces, the upper part in the form of an apron fitting closely to the figure, and the lower part, being cut so as to hang full round the feet. The double-breasted coat is cut very short on the hips, and is scalloped and braided all round to match the skirt. The collar is cut in quite a novel style, and the whole costume is lined through with rose-coloured silk. e!‘i!

Talking of the cold weather we have had, reminds me of a new evening wrap which has just ,;;Ale into use by the use af pale tints of cloth and milimere, which is specially eff2ctive when lined with glace silk in some pretty contrasting tint. White or ivory cloth looks well with a pink or yellow glace, pale grey, with salmon or daffodil. yellow, while pihk cloth and w‘hite or ivory glace are most harmonious. Green, bltia, and heliotrope should be avoided for lining, as these tints clash with so many evening tints, and few women care to have many evening wraps, and require one which is wearable with any gown. Your loving JKNNfill

"WOMEN'S CHAT."

The London season has opened with considerable dis- content, much grumbling has been the order of the day. Many of the gowns carefully thought out and completed for the Drawing Rooms have been rendered useless, owing to the order for half mourning, and in the general rush and scramble for others, women have had to put up with scant attention and consideratlon from the over- worked modistes.

A half-mourning Drawing Room is by no means neces- sarily a monotcnous,or colorless one. There are the almost endlessly varied tones of purple lilac, heliotrope, mauve and rhododendron which is nearly pink, but just saves itself by annexing the title "orchid mauve." There are also many tcnes of cyclamen which are really red- dish-mauve, these pass under the name of "cyclamen mauve." To add the name of a country to some color is sometimes sufficient to make it a mourning shade. " Tyrian purple is a case in point, but it is doubtful if such a decidedly scarlet tint would ba allowed, even in these days, as mourning, although many of the so- called shades of grief are really only fourth cousins,four times removed, to the original hue. in her aversion to conventionality, the ex-Queen of Naples has always closely resembled her sister, the late Empress of Austria. Her eccentricity once took the re- markable form of hiring the Paris Hippodrome upon several occasions, where, in the presence of only her husband and servants, she appeared dressed as a circus rider, and riding barebacked, jumped through hoops, and over banners, while the ex-King Francis threw rub- ber balls into the air, which she skillfully caught at full gallop. Her object being to prove to her own and her husband's satisfaction that she was quite as clever as any professional. Miss Clara Butt, one of our most popular contralto singers, has quite recovered from her recent indisposi- tion, and is in excellent voice again. Miss Butt is very bright, and full of fun,and has a most breezy unconven- tional way of talking. She was born in Jersey, and was prepared for the scholarship which she ultimately ob- tained at the Roy al College of Music by Mr. Rootlaam, one of the best singing masters in Bristol. Tte first im- portant appearance was at the Albert Hall (London) in December 1892. Miss Butt's voice is unique, hr com- pass extends from the low C (in the bass clef)to A above the treble stave. _ . .

_ Fashionable wediings will be no more until Easter is with us, as no one dreams of being married in Lent. The diminution in the number of brid-sma'cls kas been very noticeable of late. Ten and twelve were by no means unusual at one time, but several latter day brides have shown themselves quite content with two or three. In Paris many society leaders are ridiculing the whole idea of bridesmaids, the bridal veil, b quote, &c., and advocating the" travelling dress " ceremony what their motive is, for this change of opinion, it would be diffi- dult to say, but one thing is certain, it is not due to the desire for economy. Before Princess Henry of Battmberg introduced the MOCIO of having Princesses for bridesmaids, it was not considered right for royal ladies to act in this capacity, and the daughters of noblemen were invariably chosen. The last occasion when the daughters of the British aris- tocracy were bridesmaids to royalty, was on the marri- age of the second daughter of Princess Christian, to Ptir ce Arebert of Anhalt, neatly eight years ago.

Toques built entirely Of flowers ;re alit ady en evidence on fine days, as are also Fght, spring-like cats and skirts. To prove a success the former must be of the very beet French design, or they have an ,anpearance of vulgarity. A little white jonquil with an aigrette of its own green leaves, and a tiny knot of brilliant green velvet, makes a lovely toque for a young face. Ribbons promise to be very little used for trimming smart mil- linery, only the more simple kinds ef hats. For this purpose, quite wide ribbons will be chosen, plain and fancy sorts about With the bright warm spring weather, blouses and shirts will appear again, in full force, and a few hints concerning belts, we should many of us, do well to bear in mind. Women of rather too ample proportions should patronised the narrow belt only, and if it fastens at one side with a large bow under a slightly pouched bodice, the appearance is greatly improved. The waistband should invariably—by slim and stout alike—be pulled slightly below the waist line in front, and whenever possible, it should be composed of a yielding fabric, cut on the cross, so that it fits the figure tightly. Waved fronts and entire wigs are being worn by large numbers of women, at the present time. This is partly due to the desire to allow their own hair leisure to recover from the waving and curling operations it has so long been subjected to, and partly to the desire to save time. The fashionable woman has at least four or five wigs, and three or four knots of hair. These are dressed on blocks, and kept ready for use. Should she come in from a brisk ride, or a spell of cycling, three minutes will suffice to change, and send her to the luncheon table with a perfectly dressed head. In the evening, instead of the lengthy operations with curling and waving irons, all that is nrceseary is again to remove the wig, replace it with 4 freshly dressed one, and substitute for the low knot, a high, and more elaborately arranged specimen. This operation takes less than five minutes. and ninety-nine times out of a hundred, detection is impossible. POTTED BEEF.—This makes a pleasant change for breakfast, costs very little,and does not take much time to prepare. Take hale a pound of cold roast or boiled beef, cut it small, and pound it in a mortar. Add a little butter, powdered mace, a saltspoouful of male mustard, a few drops of anchovy sauce, pepper, salt and cayenne. When quite smooth, and ail the season- ings are nicely blended, press the paste with jars, and cover with clarified butter. Those who do not possess a mortar should buy one as soon as possible, for with it any scraps of fish, cold meat, or poultry can be conver- ted into a palatable relish. " MADGE."

LIFE SAVING IN THE ROYAL NAVY.

One of our representatives tele the story :—" Sean l- ing at the pier-bead, Tilbury Dock, waiting for an over- due boat in the recent Channel ga,e, I fell into conversa- tion with the pie-head master. Mr. J. Palmer, a fine lorliog man of perhaps forty years of age. He asked if I bad made many voyages in rough weather, and was in- terested to hear of my experience. "We have had some rough times here during the last few days, too," he said. "Will you excuse my asking, sir, what your profession is ?" I answered that I was in the proprietary medicine business. _ _ .

He immediately asked, almost anxiously, "What proprietary medicine 2" And on my Baying" Warner's Safe Cure," his face lighted up with pleasure, and grasp- ing my hand Warmly, he told me the Safe Cure had saved his life when serving in the Royal Navy, on the West Indian Station. I asked him to tell me the story, which he did most willingly. "It is perfectly true that if it had not boen for Warner's Safe Cure, I should not be standing out here on the pier, exposed to every kind of weather, and able to face it all, rough or smooth. For in 1889, while in the Navy, serving in the West Indi s, I was taken very ill, and the medicine ordered by our doctor seemed to do me no good at all. My complaint was Kidney and Liver disease, and my legs, hands and face became ter- ribly swollen. "I had read in the papers about other people who had been cured of this same disease by Warner's Safe Cure, and &though the doctor wouil not recommend it, I determined to give it a trial. I obtained a few bott'es through the Army and Navy Stores, took the contents perseverinAly, and here I am safe and sound, never baying had a day's illness during my last seven years of service here.

"I can certainly recommend the Safe Cura to any- body, and shall be only too pleased to do so." Warner's Safe Cure has be( n before the public for the past fifteen years, and achieved a success never equalled in the history of medicine, not only in Great Britain, but in the United States, Germany, France and Aus- tralia, and where it may ba obtained of all chemists. These are not idle statements, but which have been tested and proven by thousands of sufferers of all con- tinests.

SALT UNION LIMITED -ANNUAL

REPORT.

The tenth annual report and balance-sheet of the Salt Union, Limited, has been issued. There is no divi- dend on ordinary or preference shares. During the year the sum of £5,924 was expended an new work's. There is a balance of £45,323 on profit and loss aecount. De- benture stock interest amounted to £51,000, leaving a deficit of £8,676, which amount has been transferred rem the reserve fund to the credit of profit and loss cco-rnt. The tonnage of salt delivered was less than ast year by 47,000 tons. The alterations in the mode of manufacturing chemicals and the change in other indus- tries have still further decreased the demand for salt for industrial purposes and quickened competition against salt producers. To the East Indian market there has been an increased supply on lower rates of freight during the last half of the year, the ben— fits from which have not yet been realised in the accounta, whereas the latter have suffered from th,e losses incurred by earlier shipments made at high rates of freight. A disused set of salt works belonging to the c*mpany have been fittei with soap manufacturing plant and machinery, and the com- pany is now producing high grade soaps for household and toilet purposes. Some of tfie craft continue to be profitably ntilised for the conveyance of general mer- chandise. The finarc,ss of the company have been some- whatbampered by the past policy of buying up proper- ties, and the Board intend to sell any surplus property as opportunity offers, reserving the brine, rock salt, and minerals, and guarding against the use of the lands for competing purpesea. In August last the salt manufac- turers in the Aliddesborough district included in the Union established the North Eastern Salt Company, Limited, which is now in sucessqlul operation. ..Sr(ni!- rus efforts have been made to form a stul,r ccin, a ny• amongst the manufacturers in the Ch,,, hire, Stiffotd- ehite, and Lancashire diE Huts, hut it 119. s not yet b2en found pcsaible to brinr.,s this about. PRINCESS DELILAH.

By MRS. GEORGE CORBETT,

Author of "When the Sea Gives up its Dead," "The Prince Physician," "The Vindication of Olive Trevethick," "The Star of Yukon," "The Adventures of an Ugly Girl," "Deb o' Molly's," "A Fair Blockade Runner," &c.

CHAPTER XIII.

LORD ALFRED SETS HIMSELF A TASK.

As a inatter of course, Lord Alfred immediately secured a copy of the paper whose contents were heralded by the sensational placards, and it needed only a glance to convince him that there were some very material points of resemblance between the disappearance of the great stockbroker, and that of John Mills and Teddy Quicke. Polly was equally interested with himself, for it had also instantly occurred to her that even the reporters were connectinc, the present mystery with the former ones; and she eagerly seconded Lord .Alfred when he expressed a resolve to begin a course of systematic investigation at once. "I will just escort you along Piccadilly to your house," he said, "and then I will set about making certain inquiries which appear to me to be exceed- ingly important. .It is not that lam especially interested in Mr. Mosenthal himself, but I am convinced that a solution of one disappearance will lead to the unravelment of all mystery connected with the others, and in this case. the scent lies hot, for there has been no serious wa3te of time."

"Go at once," urged Polly, "and see what you can do in the matter. I won't take you home with me, because the sooner you begin your investigations the better. Besides, it has struck me all at once that it will perhaps be just as well if we do not let certain people. see us often together, for much will depend on preventing them from guessing that we are working in collusion against them. _ _ _ _ _ _

Lord Allred saw the wisdom of the idea at once.

Nay, he even went a step further, and it was hastily arranged between the lovers that until certain aims had been realised, they would, to all out- ward appearances, be mere acquaintances; even going so far as to repudiate any suggestion of a possible nearer relationship. By thus practically disavowing any community of interest, it would be much easier for Lord Alfred to be successful in his task, if, as both he and Polly suspected, the Duke of Hapsburg Solltesein was a prime factor in bringing about the disappearances. The Royal Academy, being so near her home, was a very convenient place for Polly to select as a rendezvous, but, Ter cuntra, it had the dis- advantage of being too fashionable a resort to escape the unwelcome intrusion of the very individuals whom it was desirable to mislead. It was, there- fore, hurriedly arranged to substitute the British Museum as a meeting-place whenever it seemed unsafe for Lord Alfred to appear openly as a visitor to the Rizzen-Quickes. Any important communication which Polly might have to send was to be addressed to the Junior Carlton, and Lord Alfred would supplement his occasionl visits by daily letters to his fiancée.

Then Polly huiyied liome, and• her-lover entered a hansom in which he was driven to Scotland Yard, where he asked to see Detective Raleigh, who luckily chanced to be on the premises at the time. To him Lord Alfred explained as much as he considered advisable; and then gave a concise summary of what he believed to be a sequence of events which were the work of a gang of con- spirators. "The first link in this chain of mystery," he said, "is the disappearance of Mr. John Mills, together with a large sum of money which he had just with- drawn from his bankers. A few weeks later, Mr. Edward Rizzen-Quicke also vanishes, also with a freshly-drawn sum of money in gold; and it must not be forgotten that Mr. Mills was intimately connected with the Rizzen-Quicke family, thus justifying the theory that we must look for the culprits among people who are acquainted with the regular habits of the family which seems to be especially singled out •for sinister experiments. Soon afterwards there is a daring robbery at Mr. Rizzen-Quicke's house, and one of the most unique jewels in the world is taken away. But for a fortuitous cab accident, there can be no doubt that Mr. Rizzen-Quicke, senior's, disappearance would also have to be recorded; and now all London is placarded with news to the effect that Mr. Mosenthal, the well-known stockbroker, has been spirited away.r. There is evidently a deadly system of kidnapping at work, of which the motive is clearly apparent to the simplest mind, for in each of the three first cases a large sum of money has disappeared; and I mean to find out how far the analogy has been continued in the case of Mr. Mosenthal."

"Jean enlighten you a little already on that point," put in the detective quietly, "for the first idea which suggested itself to me in connection with Mr. Mosenthal was to ascertain if he had followed the example of the others in disappearing with a lot of gold in his possession."

"And has lie ? " "Yes: This time the number of sovereigns in- volved is not less than six thousand, for Mr. Mosenthal had running accounts with the Union, the City, and Lloyd's Banks; and his withdrawals have been judiciously divided between the three. Of course, it can readily be understood why the victims have been induced to load themselves with sovereigns, for these cannot possibly be traced; whereas paper currency of any sort cannot be used without almost certain detection. The most vexing part of the business, to my mind, is the ease with which that cabman got off with Mr. Rizzen-Quicke's thousand pounds. his capture would have been of great value to us; for the man who drove the hansom which could not be identified as anybody's property is either one of the principal conspirators, or he is hand and glove with them." "And how do you account for the facility with which all these men have been induced to fall into the traps that have been laid for them? " "Well, my lord, I can only think of one explana- tion, but I fancy it will prove to be the correct one. They have been hypnotised; and that man who pre- tended that I had advised him to go to Mr. Rizzen- Quicke is the hypnotist himself." "You are right! That is exactly the conclusion I have arrived at. But, having learnt so much, of course you have been doing your best to trace that man ? "

"Of course I have, my lord, but I have not found him, although his description is posted in every police-station in the kingdom." "I can tell you why you have failed: you are looking for a stout, elderly, bearded man, possibly forgetting that the very nature of his errand would suggest the necessity of adopting a disguise. But would you be surprised to hear that I think I can put my finger on him ? " Needless to say!' Mr. Raleigh was very much sur- prised, and he eagerly agreed to second the efforts of the young aristocrat., who was making it his personal business to unravel mysteries of a peculiarly uncanny nature. The task upon which Lord Alfred entered was a dangerous one; but the arrangements which he made with the detective shewed that the criminals, whoever they might ultimately prove to be, would have as an antagonist a young man who had every element of success in his favour. He was full of mental and physical vigour; had plenty of money at his disposal ; and was so level-headed that not a single precaution was neglected by him. Mr. Raleigh's name had been mentioned by the man who undoubtedly hypnotised Mr. Rizzen- Quicke, and it was, therefore, palpably unwise for him to be openly enlisted in the work of elucidation. So he was deputed to communicate with Lord Alfred by letter only. Another detective was engaged as personal attendant to Lord Alfred; and to a third man was to be entrusted the duty of shadowing his lordship, whenever he took his walks or drives abroad.

This duly appointed guardian was on no account to betray his interest in his charge by addressing him, unless the safety of the latter made such a course appear necessary. Any information he might want to impart was to go in a roundabout way, either through the new valet or vid Mr. Raleigh at Scotland Yard. In this way Lord Alfred hoped to secure his own safety, and to avert all risk of being suspected of cherishing too great an interest in the urravelment of the kidnapping mysteries. By the time he and D,Jective Raleigh had come to a thorough under.Aanding with each other, Lord Alfred began to feer the need of physical refresh- ment, and he drove straight from Scotland Yard to his club, where he dined leisurely, the while he assimilated the opinions of various gentlemen, whose conversation dwelt largely on Mr. Mosenthal and his affairs, "There will be atma • tio Stork Exchamze to- man, who had e conversation wi" anwars th

"Indeed!" remarked Lord Alfred meditatively. "Am Ito infer from you that you believe Mr. Mosenthal simply to have absconded with other people's money ? " "Well, that is putting it rather too strong! But 1 don't mind betting a fiver that what I have hinted at will prove to be the true solution of the affair. Why, you have only to look at the prospectus of the company, to be brought round to my opinion." "Can you give me the chance of looking at a vrospectus ? I have a hazy idea that I was ria•cularised on the subject myself, but I did not esrx much attention to it."

f'ails invited, Lord Alfred's voluble acquaintance withdrew from his pocket a printed document, which proved to be the prospectus of the .Electrolytic Gold-maker's Company, and a rapid glance over its preliminary list of shareholders made the peruser feel astonished at the number of financial gulls that were evidently waiting to be plucked by the unscrupulous promoters. The list of those persons who figured on the board of directors was rich in titles, but it bore no names of established repute in the financial world. The prospectus itself was a glowing literary effort, containing golden promises enough to extract hundreds of thousands of pounds from such unwary investors as were unable to discriminate between the false glitter of names which were all show, and the reliability of more modest promo- tions, which were godfathered by men who did not care to lose their hardly-won business reputations. Two names which Lord Alfred had half expected to see were absent from the prospectus. But this was no proof to him that their owners were not mixed up with Mr. Mosenthal and his affairs.

"The Duke and the Prince," he told himself, "are supposed to be people of unlimited resources, and there is no apparent reason for connecting them with the financial speculations of Mr. Mosenthal. Yet. various little circumstances seem to point to a different conclusion, and I have more than a suspicion that they are nothing better than -nld adventurers, who keep up 'their glittering ;retensions by means of constant scheming of a shady nature. I am sure that Prince Carlos is wooing Polly for her money only. Certainly she is sweet enough to be worth wooing for herself, but she is not the kind of girl who would appeal to a man of such a disposition as I credit him with possessing. On the principle of 'Birds of a feather flock together' I should be inclined to distrust the Duke of Hapsburg-Solltesein, even if I had not already become convinced of his insidious use of a dangerous power. Now, if I take it for granted that the Duke and the pretended detective are one and the same person, it will be easy to go a few steps further, and argue the matter to its climax. Thus it would seem that the man who almost succeeded in spiriting away Mr. Rizzen-Quicke was the same individual who did actually spirit away Teddy and Mr. Mills. Nor can there be any doubt that Mr. ,Mosenthal's disappearance is due to the same agency, and was dictated by the same motives. In all these cases the object in view has been speedy enrichment, for in each and all of them there has been involved the vanishing of a large amount of money, which, being in sovereigns, could not be traced. Granting, therefore, that the acquisition of money is the primary motive, other forces besides hypnotism have been at work, especially where Teddy Quicke was concerned, and I should very much like to discover to what extent the Princess Delilah is interested in the successful sult of this kidnapping campaign. My justifica- __on for assuming that the Princess is one of a trio of daring criminals may seem very slight, but great conclusions have, ere now, been built up from equally small foundations. 'Moreover, I mistrust the woman, and I am no judge of character if she is not a scheming adventuress. In any case, I can prove the soundness of my theory by pretending' to fall under the spell of Princess Delilah's beauty. I shall then know what sort of influence she brings to bear upon her lovers."

CHAPTER XIV.

II VII THE PRINCESS DELILAH. It was half-past nine in the evening, and the Princess Delilah's residence was the scene of quite a lively gathering of friends, who had called to inquire after her welfare, as a result of the alarming paragraph which had appeared in some of the evening papers. To all appearances there had been slight foundation for the rumour which credited her with having met with an accident worth recording. All that had happened,she said,was that she had stumbled when coming out of a carriage, had put out her hand to save herself from falling, and hid sprained her wrist a little. There had been no need to call in the services of a doctor, for her maid had bandaged her wrist for her, and it would very likely be all right again in a day or two. Well, I'm sure I am glad to hear such good

.we," observed Mr. Price, the wealthy American who had figured at Princess Delilah's ball as one of her honoured guests, and who was now apparently on terms of intimate friendship with her. "When I read the paragraph I got a little fright lest something had happened which would cause London to lose its brightest star." "Now then, Mr. Price," pouted the Countess Sforza, who was also present. "I think that was very ungallant of you. You should have remembered that you were in my company, and have said one of its brightest stars! ' " Ah, my dear Countess," interjected the heroine of the evening, "you don't need to grudge me my poor little triumphs, for all the world has heard of yours, and if reports speaks truly, you cLut become the wife of a Prince of Royal blood, if you like." "Perhaps it is lucky for the Prince of Royal Blood that I have no inclinations in that direction," was the smiling rejoinder. "I suppose the gentle- man you mention labours under the impression that he would find in me a docile wife, who would be ecstatically grateful to him for his condescension in marrying her, and who would be only too pleased to put her fortune unreservedly into his hands in exchange for the barren honour of becoming the left-handed consort of a worn-out roue. No, no, the temptation is not strong enough for me."

"I commend your discretion, my dear Countess,* roved the Princess, with a sweet smile which 4tned to indicate the greatest admiration for her lovely Hungarian friend. "Your beauty and grace, especially when enhanced by those wonder- ful rubies of Fyours, are much more [potent while you are free to control your own actions, than they would be if you were subjected to all the ceremony and restrictions which would hem you in if you were to become the wife of a Prince, however exalted."

Mr. Price was not sure that be was enjoying himself, for a three-cornered conversation it; not always of the most entertaining kind, and he felt greatly relieved when three more visitors were announced in rapid succession, these being Prince Carlos of Mitylene, Lord Alfred Vavasour- Pomeroy, and Lady Flora Vavasour-Pomeroy, all of whom had come to ascertain the extent of Princess Delilah's injuries. -

A glance at the "radiant creature herself was almost sufficient answer to each solicitous inquiry, for nothing could be seriously amiss with a woman who looked so beautiful ana so happy as she did. A glance, also, told Lord Alfred that everything was favourable to the keen scrutiny to which he intended to subject Princess Delilah, for We more people there were in the room, the more unlikely was it that she would be able to bestow much attention upon him in return, and, although he d not taken his sister entirely into his confidence, -fa had enlisted her services so far as to induce her

egompany him to-night for the express purpose -2.tmg herself pleasant to anyone who might u-nerwise wish ito monopolise too much of the Princess's conversation.

Lady Flora's sisterly faith needed to be quite as strong as it was, to be proof against the, natural suspicion that her brother was wavering in his love to Miss Rizzen-Quick-e. But she knew that he was not given to deceiving her, and when he told her that it was to Polly's interest for him to spend a pleasant evening in the society of a much more beautiful woman she never doubted his assertion foe. a moment, far-fetched though it seemed. t- "tVhat is all right," she said to herself, as she toot ready stock of the visitors who were already there. "If Prince Carlos will only talk to me, and if the American gentleman will make himself happy with the Countess, that will leave Alfy free to take all the notes he requires, though what he intends to make of them I can't imagine. There now, here comes another visitor. I expect that will upset Ally's calculations a little." The other visitor proved to be the Duke of Hapsburg-Solltesein. But so far from being _ . .

annoyec as an a;

.ftt the II(

avasion, Lord .Alfred hailed it and silev.ed himself

the Dime with a milcl toleration towards him whICII had not hitherto existed.

"So the young fool has turned moth, after all, ad is singeing his wings as recklessly as many another unsuspecting votary of love has done! fancied that he was bending at Miss Rizzen-Quicke's shrine, but if he once feels the spell of Princess Delilah's power, the Gold King's daughter wilt soon be out of the running, unless his lordship jo of a mercenery character, which I doubt." "If those two fellows have been to see the Itizzen-Quickes since dinner-time," thought Lord Alfred, "they have soon terminated their visit, and I hope they didn't succeed in doing any mischief while they were there." _

Prince -Carlos had good reasons for knowing that he had a rival for Polly's hand in Lord Alfred, and if he had obeyed his first impulse he would have scowled angrily at the said rival. But he was one of those lucky mortals who generally have their emotions under complete control, so far as the outward demonstration of them is concerned, and he was affability itself when, in due course, he exchanged greetings with the young English noble- man.

The latter had come here for the purpose of exercising his observant powers; but he knew very well that the people whom he had now encountered would subject him to strict scrutiny in return, and it required all the finesse of which he was capable to enable him to simulate unconsciousness of the fact.

Yet he was successful in doing so; and, by way of interlude between social amenities, he made good use of his opportunities to pursue the task he had set himself. He shewed himself so enthralled by the Princess Delilah's beauty that Mr. Price became positively jealous, for the latter could not know that in this case the Princess was the subject of a species of mental inventory, instead of the object of Lord Alfred's ardent admiration. Lord Alfred himself summed up the lady's various "points" as follows:

"Item, a tall, voluptuous figure, with features containing no physical, but many moral blemishes; item, brilliant colouring, such as pale Olden hair, lily white skin, delicately rosy cheeks and lips, and heavily fringed dark blue eyes; item, exquisite dress of pale sapphire - blue chiffon, trimmed with; innumerable pearls; and, most prominent item or all, the wonderful star-sapphire." "I suppose it is not possible," he thought, "that that can really be the same stone which Mrs. Rizzen- Quicke lost, for it seems preposterous to imagine that the Princess Delilah did not acquire it in the, manner she mentioned. Besides, she had stated weeks previously that she was anxious to become the possessor of the only other star-sapphire which was known to exist, and she was actually wearing this one before the burglary at Mr. Rizzen-Quicke'a. house took place. On the other hand, I have Mrs. Itizzen-Quicke's own statement to the effect that the uncertainty about the fate of its donor had invested it with such painful associations that she had not even looked at it for some time before it disappeared. If, therefore, I assume that the rest- less star was already out of Mrs. Rizzen-Quicke's possession before the night of the burglary, it is also safe to assume that the burglary itself was merely a cunning ruse which was intended to: establish the opinion that Mrs. Rizzen-Quicke's star-sapphire did not leave her house until after: the Princess Delilah had publicly shewn herself tij be the owner of one like it. However, I can soon' settle that question by interviewing Mr. Stree himself. I will go to him to-morrow morning, and find out if he really disposed of a star-sapphire- within the last month. By the bye, what is the! matter with the Princess Delilah's thumb ? " This last question was prompted by a striking peculiarity which had hitherto escaped Lord Alfred's notice. The beautiful Princess seemed., never for one moment to forget that it was her mission to charm the hearts of men; and she was: perpetually solicitous lest her tout ensemble should suffer in the smallest detail. Hence, she had, acquired the trick of allowing her right hand to wander over her dress, among her hair, and among: her jewels, to assure herself that not a bow, or a" jewel, or a curl had become disordered. It was while she thus manipulated the pale blue ostrich'. aigrette which crowned her wonderful hair that Ow said peculiarity attracted Lord Alfred's attentionp He noticed that she practically made no use what 4 ever of her thumb, and that, to all intents and purposes, it was an idle member. The study of mental science was a passion with him, and he had no difficulty in forming his own conclusions concerning the sinister meaning of an apparently unimportant habit, for he recalled the following opinion of a well-known brain specialist "There is one infallible test, either for the - approach or the presence of lunacy. If the person whose case is being examined is seen to make no - use of his right thumb; if he lets it stand at right angles from his hand, and employs it neither irt salutation, writing, or any other manual exercise, you may set it down as a fact that that person's. mental balance has gone. He may converse in-. telligibly ; may, in every respect and with the utmost care and cunning, be guarding the secret of a mind diseased; but the tell-tale thumb will infallibly betray the lurking madness which is cons eealed behind a plausible demeanour." "H'ml That settles it!" reflected Lord Alfred,' to whom the discovery had come with something like a shock. "My instinct did not lead me astray; and I was not mistaken when I concluded that the Princess Delilah was capable of resolving herself' into a dangerous person. Now I happen to know that Teddy Quicke saw a great deal of her; and it - would not surprise me to learn that he was here- the evening before his disappearance. The Princess was wrong the people who disclaimed having seen him for several days; but physiognomical observa.e tion tells me that lying is second nature with her, so I don't need to believe that statement. I feel less sure of her intimacy with Mr. Mills and Mr. Mosenthal ; but I do know that they were both acquainted with her, and that the latter at least was on visiting terms with her. lam also con- vinced that her relations with the Duke of Hapsburg: Solltesein and his friend, the Prince of Mitylene, are more cordial than would appear on the surface;: and I will conduct my preliminary investigations on the hypothesis that I have to deal with a trio of schemers who are responsible for the abduction of several persons, whom they knew to be in the. possession of money." Having thus worked out the whole thesis of the case to his own satisfaction, and having, he believed, done much towards making the Princess Delilah. imagine that he was becoming infatuated by her beauty, Lord Alfred hinted to his sister that it would soon be time to take their departure, and was on the point of rising from his seat, when he made a curious discovery. His right hand had been carelessly playing with the cushions of the luxurious chair in which he had been sitting, when it came into contact with something which wa.s. hardly to be included in the everyday economy of rt drawing-room lounge, and his fingers instinctively closed over it. He wanted to examine it before he allowed anyone else to see it; for he was on the look-out for traces of the recent presence of either Teddy Quicke or of Mr. tiosenthal ; and the nature of the object which was now in his grasp was such that one surreptitious glance at it convinced him that he had made a valuable find, which by no - stretch of imagination could be assumed to be the- legitimate property of the owner of the room. Before Lord Alfred rose from his seat, he had slipped the newly discovered article into his pocket without being observed by anyone else, and he was so cautious that he waited until he had seen his sister safe home, and until he was secure from observation in his own room before he removed it from its hiding-place again. Then he was able to - scrutinise his find more closely, and the scrutiny confirmed the hasty opinion he had already formed. "Well, I must say that if the Princess Delilah is not one of the confederates of our hypnotising friend, circumstantial evidence is playing her some strange tricks; for if Mr. Mosenthal has not been in her rooms so recently that even the cushions of the chair on which he must have sat have not been disturbed, how des it happen that his seal and part of his watch chain were there? I am sure this is the seal I saw him wearing at the ball, for. I noticed it particularly, as I considered it much too showy and bulky for a gentleman to wear nowa- days. But it will not be so easy, without accepting the theory of violence, to explain how the watch- chain to which the seal is attached, had been subjected to such a strain as to break it in two."

NEWS OF THE GAME LIE ENGLISH CHAMPION AND BLINDFOLD CHAMPION OF THE WORLD, IN WARRINGTON.— Local players were given an especial treat in simultaneous blindfold play on Friday last, in Cairo-street School-room, when Mr. W. Gardner, of Leeds, "The English Cham- pion," engaged 12 of the strongest players of our town, simultaneously and blindfolded. It is very Pleasing to note the immense interest which is being taken in the game in local circles. One is almost led to think, judging from the number which assembled in Cairo-street School-room,to wit • meta Mr. Gardner's exhibition, that one-half the population of Warrington are draughts- players. However, be this as it may, it was no doubt very gratifying and pleasing to Mr. 40-ardner, to find himself and his play- ing, welcomed and appreciated by the people ,of Warrington. Prior to the commencement of the saxbibition.the "Chalmers Cup" (Warrington League Trophy) was on view, and as Mr. Chalmers, the generous donor of the trophy, was present, Mr. F. Dunne evidently thought it a fitting opportunity for the Draughts League to express in some measure its thanks to Mr. Chalmers for providing them with such a magpificient and valuable trophy. Accord- ingly he proposed that the best thanks of those 'Present be given to Mr. Chalmers. Mr. F. G. Taylor seconded the motion, which took the form of a hearty round of applause. Mr. Chalmers having suit- ably responded, the exhibition began. Mr. Gardner Played the black pieces at the first six boards, and White at the rest. By this, any player who had a 'Partiality for any particular colour could have his .choice. During the progress of the games, Mr. Gardner, frequently elicited applause by naming the squaves upon which the men, in several ot the games, were situated. After about two hours' play, Mr. Gardner had succeeded in winning nine out of the twelve games played, the remaining three being drawn. Score

THE PEACE CRUSADE.

===== 0002601_18990224_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
A GLEAM OF HUMOUR.

-

BY W. T. STEAD. ,

There is no stay in the progress of the Cruse& of Peace. The opening of Parliament and similar matters may have temporarily displaced it from the position it held in the columns of the news- papers, but the movement marches along irre- sistibly. Every week there is issued to the members of the General Committee a list of the meetings held and to be held. From one page of printed foolscap that catalogue has grown to nearly three pages. Yet it does not take cognisance of half the gatherings. With scarcely an exception the meetings noted are town's meetings. The smaller gatherings which are not called by some local authority are left almost unrecorded. The more one sees of the great provincial centres the more one becomes convinced that the feeling in favour of peace is such as would never be con- jectured by those who seldom leave the capital. London is doing well, better than it has done for any like idea for many years, but its efforts seem Cold and formal beside the burning enthusiasm of many of the great centres of population which I have been visiting. Lancashire has given its enthusiastic co-operation to the Crusade. Man- chester, Lancaster, and Batley have all spoken with strong voices. The meeting at Batley, although held in a chapel and on a Sunday, was a town's' meeting, and one of the most thorough-going in its adherence to the Czar's proposals that we have had. So intensely serious is the general note of the Crusade that anything which lightens the general earnestness of purpose is welcome. In the early days of the movement the Bishop of London and myself were bombarded with texts from Scripture. That form of amusement appears to have palled on the too-diligent students of their Bibles. But the same spirit was shewn at Grange-over-Sands on Thursday. The splendid meeting there listened with amazement and amusement to the letter of a clergyman, who said he could not attend the meeting because, according to an Isaiahan text, only when Jehovah judges in the midst of the nations can there be universal peace. Another man was unable to support the movement because the Scripcures speak of wars and rumours of wars, and if the Crusade was successful the Scriptures will be convicted of a mistake.

I am writing before the holding of the confer- ence of London Labour leaders in St. Martin's Town Hall, and it is only possible to say, of it that the omens point unmistakably to a remarkable gathering of much of what is best in the working- class movement in the South. An admirable group of speakers has been got together, and among the others who will be present are Mr. Thomas Burt, Mr. Charles Fenwick, and Mr. Fred 31Taddison, three of the men who so worthily represent their fellows at Westminster. Nothing has been more gratifying than the honest, whole-hearted support accorded to the peace movement by the workers and those who speak for them. Even those who directly might seem to have much to lose from any scheme of disarmament have taken a broad view of the question. Only the other .day a Crusader jokingly asked a number of shipwrights on the Tyne how it was that he found them supporting peace. The answer came from a thoughtful man that Pr there were fewer warships to build there would be more merchant vessels. The shipwrights, he said, would not suffer. When the national convention assembles in London it will be in some respects the most notable gathering of public men which has been seen in the metropolis for a generation. . The great town's meetings are choosing their representatives at the convention with a duo regard to the great- ness of the movement they are sent to support. Here are a few of the delegates, picked at random from many scores. From Westminster will come Cardinal Vaughan, Sir Joseph Pease, and Mr. Burdett-Coutts, M.P., the representative of the division in the House of Commons. Mr. Alfred Thomas leads the Welsh party in Parliament. He is elected to the conference by Cardiff, which also sends Mr. Maclean, who is at present in India. Sir W. Hornby comes from Blackburn, Mr. W. H. Myers, M.P., from Winchester, Sir Thomas Lea, M.P.,_ from Kidderminster, and Mr. John Burns and the Rev. Guinness Rogers from Battersea. In the Same hall will be the Mayors of most of the great provincial boroughs. intim or me success of the Crusade, and more and raore as time goes on, must depend upon the exertions of the volunteers who are enrolled. Public meetings are not enough. They serve merely to shew that lie national feeling on the subject of the Czar's Rescript is real and intense. When meetings have been hold, however, in any particular town, it falls to the volunteers to see there is no back- sliding, that the aroused conscience of the place is kept on the move, and that lukewarmness is not allowed to deprive the sowing of the seed of its due fruition. There is abundant evidence that we have men who are giving themselves heart and soul to the movement. From Jersey, for example, one volunteer writes that he will undertake the organi- sation of the whole island. He has been supplied with great bundles of literature, and has disposed of a large stock of badges. Although unable myself to attend any of the meetings held in London during the week, the reports I have received shew that even in the Metropolis there is no slackening of the work of the Peace Crusade. The gathering of Shoreditch is described by the London papers, which have on the whole taken no rosy visw of the prospects of the Crusade, as "a great demonstration. When I say that the local vicar was supported by Mr. Lowles, the Conservative member for the division, the local County Councillors and members of the School Board, three or four ministers of religion (including the son of Dean Farrar), Mr. James Stuart, M.P., and Mr. W. B. Cremer, it is enough to shew that even in the course of this Crusade no such assemblage of speakers, with diverse views on ordinary questions, has come together on a single platform in the capital. Another of the great working-class constituencies In London which has held a most succissful meeting is St. Pancras. The Chairman of the 4 Vestry presided, and among the spealters were Mr. Walter Hazell, M.P., and Dr. Collins, of the London County Council. As to the future in the Metropolis, everything is now practically arranged. Tho organisers report that the whole ground has been covered, that they know of no great division in which a meeting has not been held or is not to be held. In Woolwich we are promised a lively time. The meeting there will be held towards the close of the Crusade. There is expected to be much opposition from the operatives in the great arsenal, and a speaker good at answering questions is demanded. The Vicar of Woolwich, however, is throwing himself into the task of organising a meeting, and no more fears the opposition than do we at the head ofke.

On the Continent the peace movement progresses apace. Italy has responded sympathetically to the Czar's Rescript, and will take part in the Con- ference. In Germany Henri Dunant has issued a proclamation to the peace-loving peoples. His long connection Nvith the cause of international peace enables him to speak with peculiar authority. To him we owe tho International :Red Cross Sooiety, whose beneficent work has done r4O much to mitizate the horrors of warfare. Henri Dunant was also the originator of the Geneva Convention. Like many other reformers, he hopes filet if the International Conference does not result in the adoption of a plan for the arrest of armaments it will found an International Board of Mediation. Once some such organisation is in existence, he believes that nothing can stop the development of its great work. Mr. Samuel :Rawson Gardiner, the historian; does not believe ia the Crusade. He looks to other means to bring about more friendly relations between the nations. "If we ask," he says in a letter to Mr. Bunting, "how it is that people in Yorkshire don't want to fight people in Northamptonshire, as they did in the seventh century, the answer is that they have been united by pursuing common objqcts and standing side by side against common dangers. The European Powers, I believe, can only reach peace in similar way." Surely such an organisation is this Crusade is doing something to promote that fraternity between nations which Mr. Gardines iesires.

THE MAYOR OPENS THE MEETING.

The MAYOR, who was cordially received. announced that be had received le:ters of apology for non-attend- anse from the Rev.J J. Langham, the vicar of Padgate (who expressed deep regret that he was not able to be present, as the matter had his warmest sympathy), the Rev. G. Allton, and Mr. Charles Jackson Holmes. He (the Mayor) regretted that someone abler than he was not occupying the chair, but he took a back seat to no man in his desire for the success of this great move- ment. It had his warmest sympathy. He thanked them most heartily for the excellent response they had made to the invitation. He had not had time to think much about the matter. but he could not imagine any man or woman calling themselves Christians being other- wise than in strong sympathy with this movement. (Hear, hear.) Its object was beyond the reach of politics and sectarianism. (Applause.) They should move in this matter with a strong determination to succeed, and he was sure that if they did not get all they asked for good must arise from it. Be hoped all the speakers would be given a patient hearing, and if that was done they would go home with something to think atm% (Applause.)

THE FIRST lIESOLI7TION.

The Rev. Canon Metes in moving the resolution which had been placed in his hands said he should like to say a few words of congratulation to them upon the large audience he saw assembled before him. Elo was present at very large meeting a few days ago upon the subject of temperance,ane he saw a still larger audience if possible, now gathered together. He thought they could not be too thankful to See such a large number of men and women assembled to cons der such subjects as temperance and pew), and though he could not phdg himself to everything that was said the other evening, nor perhaps could he pledge himself to ever thing that might be said on this occasion, because men viewed things very differently, still they had much in common, and he felt they had great reason to be deeply thankful when such large eemeers of Ten and women eathere I together to consider such subjects as temperance and peace. For a moment or two he would deal with the horrers of war and the blessings of peace. There was the burden of taxation in the preparation for war and the burden of taxation for the country which was defeated to pay the compensation, and then there was the great sacrifice of life and valuble property. Then again there were the terrible sufferings of the wounded, and the grief and the distress of the relations and friends of those who were killed. Those were the horrors of war. When they thought, on the other hand, of peace they could not have a better illustratton of its blesiings than by thinking of this country during the last fifty years. (Applause.) He thought they of all nations had reason to thank God for the blessings of One. What could such a conference as that which had been proposed do for them ? It could do much. First of all, the very fact rf an assembly of representatives of the different nations would bring about a better understanding be- tween those representatives and their nations. They themselves, to take a little illustration, were all the bet- ter for meeting together on that platform. They did not all think alike on many points. It, however, diel every man good to look his opponent in the face, and they were none the worse friends afterwards for having un- derstood one another. There was very much to be care- fully considered. First of all,what could be done before war, and he thought everyone would be in favour of arbitration. (Hear, hear.) Then there was the humani- tarian conduct of war, the better protection of civil life and property, and after war, merciful terms for the de- feated. It had been a strong sense of duty which had urged him to come forward and speak on that oc- casion as a minister of the gospel of peace. He would willingly have refrained from speaking, but h. felt it his duty to do so. (Applause.) He moved, "That this meeting of the inhabitants of Warrington, desires to ex- prPss its approval of the proposal of the Czar of Russia, that a conference should assemble with the objcct of seeking by international discwsion the most effectual means of ensuring to all people the benefits of peace,and tenders its thanks to Lord Salisbury for his reply to the Emperor's rescrint wherein he expresses pleasure in delegating r presenaatives to take part in the conference whenever the invitation is received." He would refer to the commer- cial and material aspect of the question. The late Lord Derby who was Prime Minister, and grandfather of the present Lord Derby, said that anyone holding his office who could not govern England with an expenditure of £50.000,000 a year was not worthy to hold office. (Hear. hear.) Well, our expenditure this year was £110.000,000. The expenditure on our army and navy had increasd by leans and bounds, until it would very soon reach the 50,000.000. That was a very serious thing. (A Voice: "What about the population ?") (The Mayor : " No interruption.") It was necessary they should defend their own shores, and it was per- fectly necessary that they should have a fleet to do so, but what he was hoping for as the result of the confer- ence was, that Powers of Europe might agree that for (say) five or ten years they would stop the increase of armies and of navies. (Hear, hear.) If that could be done it would be the most glorious event of the long end glorious reign of Queen Victoria. (Hear, hear, and applause.) He (Mr. St,al) did not think they would get the prohibition of the sub-marine boat or any arrest of armaments, but he theught they would be able at the Conference it was proposed to hold to agree to this— that before there was an appeal to the sword, or a de- claration of war was ma.de,the nations should undertake to submit their case to the mediation of some board of peacemakers to be established in the centre of Europe. (Applause.) Arbitration, he pointed eut,was not a sub- stitute for war, nor did he say that they must always arbitrate instead of fighting ; but what he did say was, always arbitrate before you fight." If, for instance. France should claim Ireland, by all means let the dis- pute go to arbitration, b?cause they knew very well that unless the arbitrator was a knave or a criminal, he would rule that France had no case and that Ireland was not to go to her. But, suppose the arbitrator was a knave, and gave Ireland te France, their position and their duty would be very plain and simple—they would fight as hard against the arbitrator as they would against France. (Cheers.) In nine cases out of ten, however, it would be found that they would not have to fight at all,end in all cases they would secure time for a consideration of their case, en-i that would be a great advantage.theimportance of which would be readily seen. He wished to ask the audience aad the people of War- rington to gather together a band of volunteers for peace —A band who would work for p.ace,,be in earnest about the matter themeelves, and then endeavour to convince their friends and neighbours that the object they had in view was an excellent one. (Hear. hear.) Lot them first make what they were driving at perfectly clear,and then bring it home to the hearts and consciences of men, and make them feel "Theta act the man." And if they did not do so they had to answer to Gad and wan for their failure. (Applause.) For three months he wanted them to help to make people as earnest about the peace of the world as they were about a football match. (Laughter.) He knew that that was a very large order, Let them canvass the town,obtain signatures to the me- morial, and he would,in fact, like to see the whole town put to the question" Do you care fr peace, or d 3 you not ?" War to many a:mote was an amusement, especi- ally when they read their morning papers, and they liked to read about bloody battles with desperate chareee. It amused them as did the actions of the gladiators in'ancient Rome amuse the Roman citizens. Other people also thought war was good for trade, while one old lady had asked, "What shall we do with the surplus pepulation if there is no war? We need war to kill them.' (Laughter.) There was a great work of missionary proregandism to be dene,and he appealed for volnnters to take up the work. (Apolauce.) The resolution was then put by the MAYOR and Carried unanimously.
