THE EX AL HN ER,

FEBRUARY 24th, 1899,

THE annual report of the The National Liberal FederatiGn. National Liberal Federa- tion is now issued, and i4t is a document of more than usual importance. In its chronicle of events will be found appreciative and copious references to the death of Mr. GLAD-. STONE, "the greatest leader the Liberal party ever had." The memorial fund will not be closed until the end of next month, and it has already nearly reached £4,000. The "free and willing offering of the pa-2ty " will take the form of a statue in the Central Lobby of the House of Commons; and Mr. PomERoY, the sculptor, is so far advanced with the work that it is hoped the unveiling ceremony may take place early next session. The Special Organisation Fund, deferred whilst so many GLAPSTONE memorial schemes were being pushed, will be proceeded with at an eirly date. The correspondence between Sir W. HA RcoußT and Mr. JOHN MORLEY is included in the report; and respecting the latter a hope is expressed that "ho may once again be found occupying a responsible position in a Liberal Administration." Sir H. CAMPBELL-BANXERMAN'S selection is cordially en- dorsed as " one which warmly commends itself to the party as a whole." A survey of the home and foreign record of. the Government will be found very pertinent and useful. Of the Irish Local Government Act it is said that the Liberals furnished the driving-power," whilst the bribe of £300,000 to the Irish landlords, by exempting them from poor-rates for ever, is held to be an illustration of " the anomalous position of the House of Lords, as con- stituted at present arid with its existing powers.' The Vaccination Act is regarded as another illus- tration of the subserviency of the House of Lords to the Conservative party. The working of the Compensation Act shows the need for better pro- vision for old age, and the levity with which mem- bers of the Government regard their election promises ; whilst the only popular reduction of taxation—that on tobacco—has been of little profit to the consumer, and may be described as practically wasted. Foreign affairs are sketched with a broad hand. Early Liberal criticisms on the Soudan advance are not inconsistent with satisfaction at the result. The CZAR'S Rescript is depicted as "the most momentous document of the nineteenth century," and realising, in " a concrete form," what Lord ROSEBERY suggested and desired in 1894. Eastern and Chinese affairs, with ref erences to West Africa and Mada- gascar, conclude the survey. The section on the by-elections is invariably readable. It is sum- marised in the sentence that " whilst the Liberal party has won eleven seats and improved its posi- tion in twenty-two constituencies, the Tory party has won three seats and improved its position in only five constituencies—all town by the way." Some slight comments are made on the pro- gramme of the `. session, and the failure to include in it the great changes contained in Mr. BAL- FOUR'S election card and Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S social programme. The duty of the Liberal party just now must be opposition and education. Under the latter head is included improved local organi- sation. The outlook is one " full of encourage- ment ''; and the Liberal party is induced to press' forward to a time when the electors will desire a change* and return to genuine Liberalism.

A PLEASANT company, a cheer- The Literation ful tone, and wise counsels Sccitty and the Church. marked the breakfast .of the Liberation Society and the Dis- senting Deputies in Landon. Members of Parlia- ment were present to shew their sympathy, and to reaffirm the principles to which many of them have been pledged, and to guide the decision of what must be recognised as the only vitalising movement in ecclesiastical affairs. The tone of the gathering was animated, and the outlook was re- garded as brighter than it had been for some time. Sir lITH.FniD LAWSON truly said that only a short time ago the Church seemed to be in an impregnable position, whereas now it was trembling by a movement from within and assailed by attacks from without, and people were discussing serious matters in a becoming spirit. He cited MATTHEW ARNOLD as a prophet who said, in 1871, that unless the Church made a blunder it might not furnish an opening for attack. That opening had now been provided. The lawlessness in the Church, the contempt of Bis- hops,Parliament, and the Privy Council, was rous- ing the spirit of England, and John Bull was de- termined to be master in his own house. Rever- ence for law was one of the highest marks of civil- isation. In France the army had assumed that it was ,above the law. In England certain of the clergy had presumed that they wore above the Church and above the State. It. was a claim that could not be admitted Parliament had many religious elements in it which he humorously described, dubbing himself "a lunatic," and hence it was to be regarded as a broad (Ecumenical Council. Dissenters were

members of it, ar,d had as much right to regulate the Church as men who never attended public- houses had to deal with licensing reform. The crisis, as Mr. OAR YELL WlLLiams declared, in. creased the difficulties of the Liberationists, be- cause liberation was getting nearer every day. Mr. HUTTON pointed out that the claims of the High Church to free government, with State privileges and endowments, could not be granted. The only alternative to State control was Dises- tablishment ; and this was made clear by other speakers. Mr. • RICKETT, indeed, raised a fresh point when he contended that the rigidity of a law-bound Church might make obedience distaste- ful; but relaxation and freedom, to be comprehen.. sive, involved separation from the State. "Protestant whirlwind," which established one ' school of thought and excluded others, might be a misfortune; but true comprehension could only come with freedom from all but religious control. The duty of Liberationists just now was to keep their main principle steadily before them, and not to lend a helping hand to the work of reforming the Church, so that. it could keep its privileges and. endowments with a minimum of State contr3l. These suggestions indicate the breadth with which the crisis in the Church is being regarded. With the Church system, as such, the Liberationists have no quarrel. As long as the Church is free, on a basis of equality with other denominations, the Dissenters have no ground to quarrel at all. What they object to are dominance, establishment, en- dowment. These are policital matters, and the Liberation Society is only a political body.

It is quite refreshing to be able to chronicle a Liberal victory in a by-election, and we congratu- late Dr. Douglas on the determined way in which he has fought and carried the Parliamentary seat for North-West Lanark. Liberals appear to have been so apathetic in the numerous elections which have taken place of late, that the spirited activity exhibited by the Radicals of the Scotch constitu- ency represented by Dr. Douglas is welcome as a draught of elixir vitae. The Liberal roll has gone up tremendously, while Mr. Graeme Whitelaw,the defeated candidate,has succeeded in improving on. , his previous total. A very close contest was ex- pected, for Mr. Douglas and Mr. Whitelaw are both young men in the thirties and are popular with the people. The progressive Views and per- sonal force of the Radical have, however, gained the day by the handsome mijority of 359 votes We hope the result is an omen of the true status of Liberalism in " canny Scotland."

Warrington's public meeting' called to consider a resolution in favour of the Czar's peace pro- posals and held on Monday evening, proved a sun.- cess complete and absolute. Parr Hail has pro- bably never contained a larger or more represen- tative gathering since its dedication to public sero vice, and this fact alone is ample proof that the subject of International Peace is one in which the inhabitants of the town take a deep interest. We do not say that all who were present attended the meeting solely because of their enthusiasm for the proposals made by the young Emperor of Russia. Many people are sceptical as to their practicability' and many others betray an apathy and lack of in- terest in the subject which is by no means to their credit. It is quite probable, nay certain, that the unique personality of Mr. W. T. Stead had some- thing to do with the dimensions of the audience, but this is none the worse for the prospects of. peace. Once under the spell of Mr. Stead's eloquence, earmestness, and optimism, and even the most stubborn of doubters is bound to admit that the case for peace rests on no unstable founda- tion.

A point on which Mr. Stead convinced all his hearers, was that in regard to the sincerity of the Czar himself. Mr. Stead had a truly remarkable personal acquaintanceship with the late Czar of Russia, and is now on similar terms of friendship with his son, the reigning monarch of that vast country. He can consequently speak with an authority which cannot be questioned as to ther characteristics of the two rulers. From him we learn that the father's constant desire for peace and goodwill to men is ever present in the mind of the son, and that it was this feeling which prompted him to issue his famous tescript. Those. who look upon the proposals with suspicion and impute unfair motives to the Emperor are thus. ' nonsuited and proveel to be in the wrong. In the further course of his address Mr. Stead drew an impressive picture of the imminent and constant danger of a universal war "in which Christian civilization itself will disappear." Of course this is a surinise,but the speaker has sifted the matter and argues with logic that such will be the inevitable outcome of the present bankrupting policy of ceaselessly increasing armaments. The bare possibility of such a disaster is terrible enough to make welcome any scheme for lessening the danger. It cannot be denied that the moment is opportune for a calm consideration of the fate to which the civilised world is being hurried by the maintenance of Europe as an armed camp, and the proposed ccnference must and will have the support and best wishes of all right-thinking, sober-minded people. Even Mr. Stead does not expect any reduction of armaments; he is doubtful of bringing the increase to a stop; but he hopeS and believes that the nations will be willing to sub— mit their casus belli to arbitration before resorting to the sword. If this much only is done it will be a great step towards peace, and the forerunner of others pregnant with still more vital issues. The resolution put before the meeting was unanimously passed, and WaTrington's quota of opinion added to the hundreds which will go to back up the re-. presentatives of this country at the forthcoming conference of the great ones of the earth.

The composition of the Australian team that ig to visit England for the forthcoming cricket season gives assurance of a stubborn contest. Noble and McLeod are new to England, and Noble brings with him a tricky style of bowling, new te most English batsmen, against whom he may, with the wind to bowl against, prove a startlingly sued- cessful opponent. '"lt is to be hoped that on our side Ranjitsinhji will be available once more. lie was the hero of Stocklan's tour, and if not tOO courteously treated on certain occasions by the crowd he never had to complain of the opposing team, most of the brilliant members of which are included in the Australian formation for '99, When Stoddart's team had to meet the hostile criticisms of the Australian crowds it waS threatened here that there would be retaliationg when the next Antipodean team visited England. We hope all animosities of that kind will be overt' looked or forgotten. The Australian papers sell like wildfire when international matches are being decided here, and unfriendly demonstrations would cause far more soreness there than was the case when the reports of Stoddart's tour were cabled to England. Probably any caution ie unnecessary. We bear the bad fortune of the game more stoically than they do abroad, and the Australians are so strong this year that we fear there will be a heavy call upon our reserves of that virtue before the season is done with.

The opening of the new works of Messrg. Fletcher Russell and Company Limited markg another epoch in the industrial progress of War' rington. The company now intend to manufacture in this town numerous appliances, the early pro" cesses of which have previously been carried otIO elsewhere. This will mean employment for a larga number of hands than heretofore and add some what to the conameicial prosperity of the Borough. It is questionable whether any to'e in the -United Kingdom has shown such untaitre takeable signs of progress as has been the case Warrington during the last few years. And the outlook is still bright. There are rumours of woe works, and farther trade developments. Warrioir ton seems destined to bo a great commercial centre, It is admirably situated for trade and commeroel and has natural advantages which many to would be only too glad to possess.

We were surprised to learn this week—and we "have no doubt many of our readers will be aimilarly affected—that Mr. Middlemore, the newly-elected member for a Birmingham constitu- ency is the seventeenth Unionist or Conservative returned to the present Parliament without a con- test, while not a single Liberal candidate has been allowed to escape the ordeal of battle. The pusi- lammity of the Liberal Party will become a by- word if this sort of thing is allowed to go on. There have been some excellent opportunities lately for the party of progress to show a little of that pluck and love of combat with which they are supposed to be plenti- fully endowed, but they have been allowed to go by the board—a policy far from being pala- table to the rank and file. Now that the party has a new, and thorough-going Liberal leader we may hope for better things. Already symptoms of increased vitality are noticeable among the oc- cupants of the Opposition benches in Parliament.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

I have another tale of woe to relate this week, and all on account of Warrington's ignoble display at St. Helens. To be beaten by twelve points to three af:er being fully confident of victory is an experience Warrington don't often endure—principally because they are seldom fully confident of victory. But on Saturday they were until about 4 p.m. when certain events had happened which caused them to think differently. The two good per- formances against Salford and Leigh had blessed War- rington with a confidence in their own prowess which they believed boded ill for S. Helens. The Saints, how- ever, recollected that they owed Warrington reparation for a number of defeats, five in succession, and made careful preparations for the fray. They left nothing to chance and succeeded in giving their opponents a Bound thrashing.

Though Warrington played shockingly, at least the 'backs did, there is some little excuse for them. To be- gin with Harris wxs forbidden to turn out by that auto- cratic body, the Northern Union, who acted upon a most absurd rule as to employment and sickness, and this considerably upset the tbreequarter line. Cross was given another trial—probably his last--at centre. and Isherwood went on the wing. Only a few minutes after the Ftart and when Warrington were pressing, Bats had the misfortune to twist his knee, the knee which was hurt about three years ago, and afterwards was of prac- tically no use. For some time he was off the field, but played through the second half with a pluck which one could not help but admire. Thus sorely handicapped Were Warrington beaten, though Ido not wish to dis- credit Bt. Helens' performance in the least.

The game was not a very exhilarating one, yet it was carried on for the most part at a very fast pace. Com- bined play was rare, especially on the Warrington side, and the shocking state of the ground militated against running and passing. I can scarcely understand how the field came to be in such a state. If it had been well 'watered, and then trampled by a here of buffaloes, it could not have been worse. S. Helens Vent in ' for spoiling tactics at firA, and then as they Baur hew disorganised was the Warrington back division they adopted short passing and quick following up With excellent results. A. penalty goal was their first point, and then the score was doubled—by a lucky drop-kick from Boyle. Before the interval Hunter scored a Meritorious try,bringing the homaiters' lead up to seven Points.

Warrington had decidedly the worst of it up to half time. but they were not beaten then by any moans, and I'light have saved the game but for a shocking mistake immediately on resumption. Stott secured, and was allowed to run right through the team and score between the posts. He ought ta have been stopped, but the Warrington men simply gaged and let him do as he liked. It was not a try like Dunbavin's of the previous Tuesday, there was no stop- Ping that,but Stott certainly should have been checked. When Foulkes added a goal Warrington's discomfiture was complete. Still they had three-fourths of the play afterwards. Fish eclred a particularly brilliant try in the corner, and saved his side from being nilled.

The Warr'ngton team was supported by a meat un- usual crowd of enthusiasts. I should think there were close on a thousand went to St. Helens by one way or another, and the like of it has not been seen befora on . any occasion this ssason. Frequently the team has gone away with less than a score of supporters, and this did not use to be the case. It was like old times on Saturday to see the crowd at Bank Quay Station. A. lot a interest was evinced in the first turn out of Wood, the Llynwypia man, at c?,ntre. lam afraid he disappointed the critics, but it is not improbable that the critics were wrong. My own opinion is that those who said he was no good are in the wrong, and will have to admit it yet. A player could not have turned out under worse conditions that Wood did on Saturday. He was out of condition through not having played for 13 weeks as the result of a broken arm ; he was nervous about that self same arm; he was strange to his club mates and to the rules; be was handi- capped by the condition of the ground and the fact that the threequarters were very effectively -smothered on this particular occasion. In the face of all this he was expected by some people to score two or three tries and altogether give a display fit for an inter- national. On the whole he did not do badly. Ho was better than Cross, and I think the committee have acted Wisely in giving him another chance against Broughtol. I 1.1. m convinced he will improve. Speaking of Crois re- me of this once capable player's deterioration. Me is playing vary poorly now, and yet trains and tries se hard as any man in the team to make himself fit. It 18 a pity he cannot get back some of his old form.

Fish was far and away the best back on the Warring- ton side at St. Helens. He played coolly and cleverly -all through, and his try was well worth seeing. With Harris on it is probable that St. Helens would have had a much harder task to keep ahead. Isherwood was of 'little use on the wing, and it would have been better 'bad he and Cross changed plaws at the interval. Ashurst was a very useful man but shone principally on the de- 'fence. Hallow, was only moderate, and did one or two foolish things. Morrison played a remarkable game -forward, and was an invaluable help when Fairhurst went half-back. He is the biggest man in the team now and works like a demon. St. Helens were best repre- sented by Stott, Liversage, and Foulkes. The diminu- tive half-back, said to weigh only 7 stones, was a con- tinual thorn in Warrington's side and bids fair to make a second Dunbavin.

" THE ANATOMY OF THE ANT."

This was -tliTe subject under disCuss.lou at the Meeting of the Field Club on Friday evening last. 34r. William Webster was the lecturer, and de3pite .the difflaulty attending a detailed description of the structure of so small a creature, he succeeded Ivith the aid of his pictures, in conveying to his audience clear ideas of the nature of the modifica- tions of both body segments and appendages which occur in various species of 811t8. He was. particu- larly happy in bringing out the facts that tne many- jointed antennm are organs not merely of smell, but of tearing, and are also the instruments by which they .cominunicate with one another. He also showed vi?ry effectively the structure of the thichea, or breathing tubes. Mr, Webster also described the structure of the poison apparatus of these little creatures. The poison glanas are always situated in the hider part of the abdomen, and in many species there is a sting, essentially similar to that of a wasp 1 whilst in those species which have not developed this organ, the jaws are powerful enough to inflict a jagged wound into which, by a rapid movement a the abdomen, a drop of the poison is forced. A lively discussion eusued. and Mr. Web- ster was warmly congratulated on his paper.

THE EXAMINER FRIDAY, FEBRUARY 24, 1989

THE LITE MR. ROBERT NORMIN.

PULPIT REFERENCE 3.

Tha pulpit at St. Paul's Weileyaa Cnapel, Reacorn, was on Sunday morning last occupied by the Rev. C. W. L. Christien (superintendent of the circuit), who de- livered an impressive sermon founded on the words "I have finished the work which thou gayest me to do." St. John XVII., 4. Having earnestly impressed his hearers with the thoroughness with which our Saviour accomplished His work on earth, the whole of His life being devoted to the deing ef His Father's will the rev. gentleman said :--Tne band of death had come among them at St. Paul's and taken some of their mem- bers away. Only a few weeks ago their dear friend 31r James eirregary was called hence. He was a most unique man in his knowledge of the Holy Word and in nis almost absolute knowledge of the Wesleyan Methodist hymn book ; and a min who loved the services of God's house. Then the hand of death came and touched tee heart of Sister Leach and she fell asleep. She had been a esisty member of the church over 50 summers andf .afiars. Se had given her heart t God at the age o 17, arid hid been a member of the cause in Heywood and Manchester. She settled in this town and for the last 37 sears had been a sincere member of the minister's class meeting, at St. Paul's, and passed away after a life well spent. In thelast few days one of their most pro- minent figures, one who had worked among them for many years, had been called away. In speaking of their friends who had departed this life they never attempted to claim perfection, for all were imperfect. But they would thank God for all the goodness He created in human hearts and lives. They would thank God for the sterling work that belonged to their departed friend Robert Norman. He was the embedyrneat of that peculiar formation of spiritual life which bad been, one of the prominent features of the Methodist Caurch from the beginning. He was born at Weston in the year 1830, and lost his father when he was only seven years of age, throuek an accident, and was left to the care of a tender mother with six others. He was converted at Weston in a prayer meeting when he was 14 years of age, and as he used often to put it "He had thus been born twice in Weston." For 55 years God spared him to show to the world how real and thorough was the change which God's grace wrought in the heart of the lad of 14 years. Now true religion always gave its possessor ever the great desire for the salvation of others. It did so in young Robert's case, and so the boy was soon found engaged in Christian work in his own native village. In 1853 he became a local preacher and was examined and elaceri on the plan by the late Rev. J. Morris. In 1859 he became a class leader. Both positions he retained to the last. He was greatly successful as a class leeder,lais classes becoming so large that they were often divided again and yet again. He became a trustee of the chapel properties. His especial work was that of a local preacher. Robert Norman was a splendid speci- men of that noble body with which God had blessed the Wesleyan Church all along. His preaching was more than the simple expressing of Biblical truths; he was warm, emotional ant dem mstrative and it was out of the depth of his heart that his mouth spoke when he occupied the pulpit. He had ti endure hardships a 3 a good so!dier of Christ, and oftentimes came home from long journeys wearied out with the day's preaching but as happy asa king. He was once arrested for preaching the gospel under,the canopy of heaven. He was not, however, a narrow bigot, but he was intelligent touce- ing the history and doctrine of his own church. He had been a most regular attendant at the services of the St. Paul's Chapel; weak day and Sabbath found him in his place, and his example in chat direction was one they should try, and emulate. At the last service he attended he was extremely interested in the proceed- ings, and on the way home expressed the hops that he would soon recover from a slight chill he had contracted,l but he had got his death chill though he did not know it; and was taken away from the presence of many of his f rivets late on Saturday evening. During his illness often ia his wandering he would burst out into song, singing his favourite hymns,which showed that the rule- ing passion was strong in him. He ;tile preacher) hoped that Gad would grant them a succession of such man in their church who, would render them such service and work as heel their dear friend Robert Norman.—The choir sang the anthem (an especial favourite of the d ceased) " The vital ere,ec of 1-1,,q,v,n1r,

J. Gros- field and Sons' works, Bank Qlay.—Mr. Browne prose- cuted on behalf of the firm, and Mr. Jenkins defended Newell. Crosfield, and the rule was that no employe should take any goods out of the works without a written permit by Mr. J4rnes Newall, chief engineer, or by Mr. W. J. Witts, storekeeper. Defendant hid previously had permits to take goods out, but on Wednesday afternoon last he ordered his labourer, Herbert Smith, to assist him in loading some timber, which they took out of the works by the ordinary ga:e. Smith believing that Newali hid got a permit. Defendant had arranged with Mr. Gittows, Man- chester-road, to build him a cycle shed, and it was for that purpose that the wool was taken. ===== 0002601_18990224_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
CHARACTER SKETCHES,

No. I.

Every person who take; an interest in the town, and especially those who have watched its growth from a mere village hamlet into a town ot con- siderable importance, cannot fail to revere those man who have materially helped it onward, and thus ,enabled.it to keep pace with the march of pro- gress. Those persons who have benetitted the com- munity cenainly deserve the highest eulogy and the warmest appreciation ot their fellow tewnsmen. Runcorn can boast of possessing many such men, who have by judicious expenditure,by well matured plans, and, it may be, by sacrifice contributed their quota to the prosperity it now enjoys; and which it not as good as desirable, is a considerable advance on the town's earlier days. There is one individual—a well-known character —whose past services are not, perhaps, recognised as keenly as they deserve. He stands out from the rest of his townsmen as one who has attained a high position by sheer hard work and dogged deter- mikation to overcome all obKacles, Rising from a comparatively low position to a lucrative post, he presents a striking instance of what an • untutored workingman can do in co-operation with other workingmen to help financially his fellows, and rise in tne social scale, at no one's expense save, his own. _ _ _ _ _

Many years ago he initiated, in conjunction with others who held similar ideas to himself—perhaps soniewhat Utopian or Socialistic—a movement which has brought happiness into many homes,and enabled the housewife to rest secure from debt,tand reap considerable advantage if only regarded from a pecuniary point of view. When, however, the social and intellectual benefits are noticed the debt of gratitude is doubly increased. Strange to say the success which attended these efforts made in fear and trembling has in no way affected the mind of this most wormy individual. He is as ready to speak to any old. comrade with waom he once worked side by side at arduous toil, as he is now to transact business with the wealtuiest. His portly figure, his jovial face, across which a good-natured smile contiaually plays;his rolling gait proclaim nim to be a typical Briton; one of the good old sort who are unhappily fast dying out. His own particular countrymen would doubtlass apply to him the ex- pression " Cymro f r Cam," watch by interpretatioa means "A Welshman to the hilt." The average Englishman would probably construe such a prase to mean "A jolly good fellow," and the canjecture would not be literally far wrong. Intensely patrio- tic he exhibits that deep and passionate pride in the land of his fathers,and in everything connected with it, which seems to be the peculiar heritage of his race. Like most Welshmen he is well grounded in religious matters, and, tnougri too broad minded to be termed a bigot,he displays all the characteristics of a sturdy follower ot Calvin. Noncon.formity is to him the ark which is to sail over a sea of dogmas and doctrines, and ultimately find a resting place in the heart of the Eternal City. Though a strong controversialist, either in secu- lar or religious matters, he has the prudence to watt until he is the party attacked, when he generally renders a good account of his opponent, being always ready to discuss any debat- able question. For a self-made workingman he is remarkably well read. Ha has a powerful retentive memory, and therefore is well informed on the majority of subjects traversed by English and Welsh literature. Welsh Hymnology is one of the favourite studies with which subject he is specially acquain- ted. His opinions are remarkable tor their candour. He is the possessor of a vigorous intellect and an unbiassed mind. Extremely cautious even to timidity, he sometimes carries his conservatism— though no anally a radical—a little too far. Though by no means witty he has an underlyinwcurrent of kumour that renders him a person if not of refined taste, an agreeable and pleasing companion. Quick to forgive any real or fancied injury, and with a disposition naturally generous the unfortunate one need never apply twice for assistance. Yet it would be strange if tnere were no "quips and cranks"aboat the worthiest members of society. In fact professors of physchology affirm that it is impossible ta find a man without some mental de- formity. The only failing —if tailing it be—that it is possible to discover in his mental equipoise is that of being a violent anti-smoker. His prejudice against what Kiagsley calla the soothing weed is of a nature at once bitter and unreasonable. Woe be- tide those luckless wights who incur his displea- sure in this respect, for a lecture of the candle char- acter is the ine fitable result. His character, or rather the elements of it by which he has achieved success form an interesting study. Push, tact, and plod have been stated to be the essentiale necessary to ensure success. If this be a truism we can hardly wonder at the result for he rossesses them in a pre- eminent degree. Doubtless method and punctuality have been also large factors. They are, indeed the Alpha and Omega of the man who seeks to woo and win fickle fortune. And the type of maa under consideration, judging from personal observation,is a noble embodiment of two of the noblest traits that it is possible for an to have. _

Our town is rich in such a character. Were there more who would display the self-sacrificing spirit which he possesses' there is every reason to eqlieve that Runcorn would be infinitely better off as a town, and the inhabitants be infinately more suc- cessful in their every-day concerns; for they would understand the value of thrift and industry which are, alter all, the chief essentials of a vigorous life in the growth of any great nation.

EPIGRAM. A man who deems that it is best, To labour for the common good; And seeks in one great brotherhood To join all ranks, that the unrest Which springs fram want by mutual aid, Be in its grave for ever laid. [No. 2 NEXT Issue.]

RIINCO RN - NOTES.

(BY A CORRESPONDENT.]

The Aged People's Treat is at present the chief charitable item to which attention is called, mid on Wednesday the Widnes Borough Minstrels are occupying the Public Hall with the commendable object of accelerating the raising of the fund.

In a short time the town well be face to face with the election ot its representatives on the various Councils. In certain quarters, especially in the Bridgewater ward,some dimsatisfaction exists as to several local questions. Well the desired oppor- tunity will come soon, and if the ward wants those grievances remedied the electors should see that their reprosentative is pledged to do something for them ; and thus secure an ardent spirit to look after their interests on the District Council. But speaking generally, there is a good deal that might be accomplished by the Urban Council all round and there is certainly plenty of scope for someone in the direction of the "assessment of works " so. as to retain as much labour in our town as possible, and not friga ten manufacturers all off:

WARRINGTON LIBERAL ASSOCIATION. ADDRESS BY MR. NI.OROJI INDIAN AFFAIRS.

A STRONG INDICTMENT.

The Co.operative Hall, Cairo-street, was well filled on Tuesday eveniag, when a public meetine was held in connection with the Warrington Liberal Association. Tire principal speaker was the Hon. D tdabhai Naoreji (formerly M.P. for Centre! Finsbury), and his subject was" India : its political and soeial c endition." Dee chair was occupied by Ceuncillor John Crosfield, J.P. He was supported on the platform by Alderman Roberts Couneillors F. R. Roberts, L. Ellison, and James Evans, Dr. Markel, Mr: J. Meuafield, Mr. Malkin. Alderman Holmes read the following telegram from Mr. A. H. Crosfield :—" Please express to Mr. Naoroji my great regret at missing the meeting and heiring him to night. I am on the sick list this week. Have vivid reeollection of bearing Mr. Niterojes mest interest- ing address at Liverpool Reform Club a few years since. Best wishes.—ARTHUR CUOSFIELD." Alderman Roberts added that as Mr. Arthur Crostield could not be preseat they could not do better than ask their President (Sir. Cresfield) to preside. .(Applause.) Mr. JOHN CELOSTIELD said he thought :they must all be about tired of seeing him take the chair. (Laughter.) He was exceedingly sorry his son child not be present for his own sake and theirs. (Applause.) He (hit son) had told him several times how much he had enj wed listeniag to Mr. Naoroji when he spoke in Limp 301. and he the (the speaker) knew it it had been possible he would have been present that night. They were going to h we a treat that night. Mr. Naoroji Was as well acquainted or perhaps better acquainted with India than any man in Eagland. (Hear, hear„) Alderman Roberts and himself had had the pleasure of an hour's conversation with him previous to the meetine and it had been most deeply interesting and instructive 0 hear what he told them about India. He told them a Lot they never knew or had had any idea of. he knew the evil of the present system of Government, and if his advice would only be listened to, India would not only be more prosperous, but would be-a much safer ceuntry than it was at the present moment. (Rear, heir.) He must centess that he himself was very ignorant about India, and he believed it would be of great benefit to all of them to hear edr.Naoroji explain difficulties and dan- gers that attached to (hat vast country. They were all anxious to hear their friend. (Applause.) 'rue Hon. Mr. NeOROJI, who had a cordial reception, said he was obliged for the opportunity of explaining the real pesition and the real relations between England and India. Lancashire was especially interested in the Indian question, and it was a very great pity that she did not understand how matters stood. Re wa9 nit present that night to be pleased with the sound of his own voice and to please the audience. Ile bad come to Warrington on a most serious business, and he had no doubt that the presence of the audience showed that they were really and earnestly anxious to hear what he had to say. He did no: ienore the good that had been done by the connection of England with India. An Indian National Cengress existed, but the Anelo-Indians tried to belittle it and run it down, and a more saicidal act they could not perform, That Congress represented the best fruits of England's connection with India. The Congress assembled at one time in one part of India, and at another time in another part, and it con- sisted of representatives and delegates from tee whole of India, Delegates from all parts of Europe met together for some cenamon purpose. That was one result of the British connection with India. Tee British had selidi- lied the Indian nation, and that wal the bsst outcome of their good work. The British had introduced into ledie—ef course at the Indians' expense—(laughter)— Western education. This Westerc education had given a new life axid new light to the Indian people: Indians had learnt from English literature and from their insti- tutions, and also that the replete were not ter the King but that the King Was for the people. They should be the servants of the people and not the masters. (Applause.) Indians of all classes and creeds,Hindoos,Maloommedans, Parsees, the richest and the poorest ,met together once a year in a conference to examine their own condition and to tell the British Government what they ought to do andovhat they had done which was good er bad. (Ap- plause.) The Indian people had been given liberty of speech, and that was the miscue they were anxious that the British connection should continue for a long time; not that they (the Indian people)professed to be benevo- lent towards the English- people. But it should be a British cennection and not an aa-Brilish one, as it was at present. (Hear, hear.) What was the good oF the English being there if India was not to derive a benefit from the British institutions? Unfortunatey there was a dark side, which destroyed the effect of the brightaide and of the connection between the two ceuntries.v In 1894, on the occasion of the Budget speech, he brought forward' a motion in the House of Commoes. and it was seconded by Mr. S. Smith, that an inquiry should be made into Indian affaire, and after he and Mr. Smith had spoken Sir Henry Fowler, Secretary for India, admitted that a spoken, indictment had been made out against the 'British rule. A. Royal Commission was appointed to investilate certain matters. The In- dians were very thankful to him for that. He would not go into all the matters, but he would laVdown this as his indictment against the present system of govern- ment. The British veeple had desired and clearly and emphatically laid it down that the Indian people should be treated and governed exactly like themselves. The Indians were asking no more than that Teey were told they were fellow subjects,and that must mean that they must be under the same form of Government. Bee were they? The British people had not performed their duty in seeing that the policy and solemn pledges had been honouraoly and faithfully carried out. That was his in- ,dictnient agaiest the whole British nation. A distinct policy had been laid down, but it had not been carrie 1 out by the servants, the executive authority. The main reason why the British people had failed to do their duty was that the Anglo-Indian. authorities in botn countries had represented all Indian matters from a one- sided and selfish point of view, had kept the British people ignorant of the true and actual state of affairs, and misled them into the delusion that all was happy and rich in India. That was the Bitish people's excuse at present—the excuse for not having looked into matters as they ought to have done. The truth of the present British rate was that it was an evil un-British. despotic, destruetive, alien eystem of the worst kind, that it was based on the principle that India must be bled or plundered, and accordingly it was un- deasingly and eveoiacressingly bled and plundered. Tae method of carrying out the evil system was political :hypecrisy and deliberate transparent subterfuge. The people of India had no voice in the administration, management, and expenditure of their own revenues and of their own affairs generally. The Indian people were not admitted into all the civil and military services in violation of the most deliberate acts and resolutions of Parliament and the most solemn proclamation of the Sovereign without distinction of race, creed, or colour. Indians, instead of being treated as Britian subjects. as most solemnly pledged, were treated a; he- lots or slaves. Generally, India was insufficiently geverned,aud, therefore, inefficiently governed. The re- sult; of the present evil system of Government were the debasement of the people, extreme peverty and terrible misery, millions of people being swept away, by famine and plague, and scores of milieu starved owing to insufficient food.(Applause.) After severs struggles with the authorities' the Indian people had obtained some smell reforms, but. the administration of the past few years had snacciaed.away even those few reforms, and the authorities were now trying to snatch away the liberty of the press and person, the higher educational services, the deprivaeien of which was one of the most dheymourable chapters ia English history (Applause.) Tune was, in fact, a general insidious eff srt to squeeze out the Indians from the higher ser- vices. As British subjects the Indoms demanded that British rule mu 4 be based on British principles and Bri- tish institutions, and Bridth citizenship, and not on the preset existing despoticem-Eaglish, selfish and destruc- tive principles, of which the pre3eat disaster and misery were the natural and hitter ftuit,and unless the present unrighteous and un-British system was thoroaghly re- formed into a righteous and truly British system des-% traction to India and disarer to the British rule must be the result. (Applause.) Toe Empire had been built up and maintained by Indian blood and money, and, more than that, India had been drained of thousands of mil- lions to the enrichment of this country, and to the impoveri-h meet of India, and yet th • only gratitude and reward was the helotry and unseen oppreesio 1 of the worst kind' Otherwise India weuld have ter-severed, and the British people would have been enriched tea times more with India's blessing and gratimde instead of her curse. A rig,tite us and honest British rule could alone be bene- ficial to India, and more than beneficial to the English people, and enormously streng'ben the foundation of the Empire in the affection and gratitude of the people. To their prayers the authorities had lent a deaf ear, and it remained for the British people alone to fulfil their pledges. There would be no longer any excuse if they did net know of these things. (Applause.) This, he placed leefore the pablic of England as his indictment. The language might be considered strong, but' he ap- pealed to them not to judge of the strength or mildness of the language from mere words, but to say whether they were true or not, and if they were true they could not be stronger. (Applause.) If they stood in his place he did not think they would be satisfied with strong language, but would go in for strong action. (Hear, hear.) The speaker went into details to prove his indictment and contended that England had never spent a single farthing in con- nection with India, and from the first day on which the British put their foot on Indian soil, the maintenance of the Indian Empire had beey entirely derived from the Indian people. It was mainly Indian blood that had formed that Empire, and the British people had received thousands of millions of its wealth. Why did they want 60 or 70 thousand soldiers there? Because they were afraid of Russia. He granted the Indians did not want Bessie. Lard Kam- b2r.ey in a speech at the Mansion Reuse sail England should never allow anything to happen to deprive

r them ef India. Yet what was good for one country WAS good for the other. John Bright had said England could net have her own good unless it was for the good of ladia. (Hear, hear.) Ile Indians could not have commissions in their army, but Eagland was very liberal with their money. Mis- sionaries were sent to India to teach them high morality yet what effect would it have on the Indian people when they saw the Government pandering to the vices of the soldiers. India was the poorest country in the world and they should have a voice in their own affairs. The Goverement of India was despotic and the Secretary oi the state for India was the Great Mogul whose will was law. Did they think the Hiadeos and Indians were such Savages and brutes that they did not understand their slavish condition? The people were reduced to starva- tion, and there were scores of millions of people who did not know what it was to have one full meal in a year. (Shame.) They were simply in a worse condition than they had ever beers under their own despots. When officers and officials retired they left the country and all the experi- ence they hal learned was lost to India. Such treatment was unworthy of the British name, and he appealed to them for their own sake to ask themselves whether they had doee their duty. Was it possible that 300,000,000 of people would remain true to England when they understood the degraded position to which they had been subjected, that they would remain for ever under the heel of the ? They tilked of the jingo rule and of expansisa but they must take care they had not an explosion in there own Empire. They could not expect that such injustice as the Indiaae had received at their hands could last long. (applause.) Councillor Ludes Evates proposed "That this meeting thanks Mr. Naoroji far his int Testing and able address, and expresses its complete sympathy with him in the ' objects of his mission." He said tnat if they had lived in India and had been treated by the Indians as the In- dians had evidently been treated by them, they would not have urged their protest so mildly as their friend had done. (Hear, hear.) Long since would Lord Salis- bury or any other statesman who was at the helm of affairs have heard about the matter. Those preseat with him must, after Mr. Naoroji's address, have a better conception than before of wha: British GovErament was in ludie. They seemed to have kaown nobody but those who lived in their own little corner of the world, but at some future time, when they recorded their votes for men to go te the House of Commons, they would re- member, he hoped, in a fuller and better sense than ever they had previously done, that they had under their Gevernment sone tlaing besides Lancashire, and that their brethern, it might be of another color of skin, should have toe same privileges and the same oppertuni- ties in lite as thly claimed for teeinselves. (Applause.) Alderman ROBERTS, in seconding the motion, said he would make a public confession that before that day he had never realised how vastly ignorant he was of the condition of things in India. They held the principle in their country that taxation and representation should ge together, and it seemed to him t be a most amazing fact that 300 millions of people should be taxed to the extent of millions a year without having the slightest voiee in the administration of the money taken out of it. (Hear. hear ) Mr. Niel' )ji had told him that the expense of the Colonial Office in London was borne by this country, but that the expense of the Indian Office was borne out of LAW!' revenue. That SB,lrred to him (Alderman Reteree) to be adding insult ta injury. It accrued to him to be the policy that the poorer the na- tion the more heavey was it taxed. It was cent° evi- dent that this condition of things could not no main- tained with seety to the great British Empire, and so much so that it seemed to him that this in atter was not a party question. It was above and beyond party. Both Lioeral and Conservative Gowerornents must have ben 'equally to blame, and he saw no reason, as he saw no reason why the Irish question should not be dealt with by both political parties—(hear, hear)—why in this question,ter eh! common safety of the Emp're,the heads of both parties sh )10 not consult to devise m lam for improving the present shameful condition of things (Applause.) If the ledians were not awarded their jest rights, military and civil, at some no distant dale they might have a tragedy far worse than the mutiny was. He hoped, however, that justice would be done, and that India would b the proudest possession of the Bri- tish Empire. (Applause.) The motion was heartily carried and Mr. Naoroji re- plied. DEATH OF WARRINGTON'S OLDEST ODDFELLOW. The Warringtoti district of the Independent Or- der of Oddfellows (Nis.nctiester Unity) Friendly Society has lost its oldest member in the death of Mr. Peter IValker, of Latchford, at the ripe old age of 84 years. The deceased joined the Constitution Lodge, which is the oldest lodge in the district, in the year 1837, and had, therefore, been 62 years a member. Owing to enfeebled health he had not during recent years taken any active part in the lodge, of which he was a past grand, lie took part in the procession of Friendly Societies which was held in honour of hr Majesty's Diamond Jubilee in 1897, when Mr. Walker also celebrated his diamond jubilee in Oddfellowship.

LECTURE ON FROGS.

" Frogs " was the subjeat ot a lantern lecture de- livered in the People's C3llege, on Tuesday even- ing in connection with the evening continuation classes, by Mr. E. Lowe, of the Museum. The lan- ternist was Mr. Hail, headmaster. Mr. Lowe said. the life of the frog was a very remarkable one. He explained the differences between the frog, the toad, acid the newt; they all had a back bon, but neither of them had ribs. They all belonged to the same family,and went under the name of batra- chians or amphibians. In concluding, he said he hoped that now when his hearers were walking by ponds, &c., they would think of the many livts that were being spent there, and take an interest in them.

CHINA TEA. SERVICES, Dinner Services, TolTet and Trinket Sets. CheAnest honse.—J. S. IikRLAND, Furnishing Ironmonger, Bride-street, Warrington. VARIETY ENTERTAINMENT AT PARR HALL.

Oa Tuesday, Viredneslay and Thursday evenings, Warrington people had opportunities of seeing the *ell-known; Barger family in their entertainment at Parr Hall. The family are certainly clever and provide an excellent entertainment, yet, Mr. John Barger is indisputably the chief attraction. His vi-n-riloquial sketches are fine examples of their kind, and are given without any artificial aids. His chief site,ch is one in which he personates a rail- way guard and alters his voice to suit the different passengers he is supposed to be conversing with.' This was a complete success. His rapid changes and impersonations all combined to make hie visit to the town, one to be remembered. The sons, To and Norman. also gave some excellent" turns." Young Mr. Toni is a promising comedian, and Mr. Norman Barger was highly applauded for eccentric comic songs and high kicking. Miss Beta Barger sang some capital songs, and pleased the audience her skipping rope and skirt dance. Mrs Bar- ger accompanied on the piano with ability, and played one or two solos with good effect. The visit has been a pleasurable one, and is an example of a Variety performance without vulgarity. PUBLIC BENRFIT U ,DER THE CLOCK ran BEST BOOTS IN LA.NCaSIIIRE. -Advt LECTURE ON "MARRIAGE."

A large number of young men gathered in the Part Will on Sunday evening, to hear the conclud- ing lecture in the social purity movement, inaugu- rated by Mr. A. ILNlirindle,secretary of the Warring- ton f.M.C.A. The full attendance must have been very gratifying to the lecturer. and was in itself an evidence of the need for the lectures. For the third time, the Rev. J. Francis Matthews presided, and, after devotional exercises, he addressed the meet- ing. The reverend gentleman pointed out that Mr. Windt..., was announced to deal with the subject of " etarriage,” and it :night strike some of them ae somewhat curious that an unmarried man, such as the lecturer should attempt to deal with a subject that only married men could adequately treat. However, Mr. Wiadle intended 'to deal with the physical aspects of marriage, and, judging from the way in which the previous subjects had been dealt with, there was no doubt that they would all leave that pace greatly benefited by that. night's address. He went on to remark that as young men required their future wives to come to them in ell the ful- -1 ness of purity, to women had a right to insist that the men of their choice, instead of being washed , out debauches and roue3 should be persons of like purity to themselves.—The Lecturer, who was warmly greked, stated that. although it might be urged that, as an unmarried man, he was not the person to deal with such a subject as marriage, yet , when they came to consider the matter carefully,. ( they would see that that fact was no bar to his efforts to enlighten them. When they were seized with any serious malady they did not argue he to the doctor's qualifications to deal with the disease before they accepted his services. The phases of the subject which he should treat were such that every man, before he married, ought to know, and he was surely qualified to speak on those top;ce Ile went en to show that if people wanted healthy offspring, the parents themselves must be healthy, an Ihe pointed out that the children of healthy parents had naturally the best chances of life, as compared with the ering of less robust people. The cry was" educate,"" educate," but all , the education in the world would not suffice to ; make amends for the physical and moral defects of ' puny children. Jr, therefore, rested with young men to see to it that they so conducted themselves in their early moneood as to ensdre, in the event of their marriage, healthy descendants, and theslee- turer urged his bearers te self-control, and to the avoidance of everything in the way of food and drink that would tend to stimulate t isir amatory propensities. He briefly alluded to the abuses of the married state, and said that his desire was to elevate their theughte floin sensual things to those of a higher nature, and he trusted that the lebtares would be the means of increasing their regard for their own body, of enligh:eniug teem as to its inactions, and of helping them to have greater con- sideration for the loveliest of God's handiwork— woman. It ought to be mentioned that throughout the lectures the organ was ably presided over by Mr. B. Wadsworth.

BEFORE the MAGISTRATES.

AN lINIIAPPY MARRIED LIFE

At the Warrington Borough Poice Court, on Friday, before the Mayor (chairman), and other magistrates, John Henry Lea was charged with committing an aggravated assault on his wife Eiizabeth Lea, on January 29th.—Mr. A. Browne appeared for the complainant, and Mr. W. H. J. Jenkinq reprrsented the defendant.— Mr. Browne said the parties were married in 1891. Last Sunday fortnight when the assault was committed the defendant had been out all day. Complainant, who had been to her mother's, returned about 20 minut-s past 10 o'clock, and he came in the house drunk. -After she bad gone to bed he gave her a thump in the eve and said he would take her money. He struck her and gave her two black eyes. When she tried to prevent him he Struck her about the body FO that she had to go to a docter.—Mrs. Lea bore out her solicitor's statement. —By Mr. Browne: He had slept with a pruning knife under his pillow and threatened to kill her with it.—Mrs Maddock, Clegg-street, and Mrs Annie Flannigan also gave evidence.—The defendant who elected to give evidence said he wao sober when he came home about 10.30 and his wife did not come in until 12 o'clock. She got the black eyes and bruises by strug- gling with the lamp and the fender with him, but he did not strike her.—Mr. Jenkins submitted that the wife had brought those quarrels about by her temper.—Tne magistrates found there had been an aggravated assault and fined defenthnt 10; and costs or in default 7 days. —Evidence was then given for a separation 'order which was granted with the custody of the children and 93. per week.

At the Warrington Borough police Court on Monday morning before Alderman Sutton (in the chair)and other magistrates, Edward Connor, cab and 'bus proprietor, Sankey-street, was charged with wilfully obstructing the free passage of the thoroughfares in King-street, Mill-street, Golborne-street,and Mount-street, by placing a number of cabs and 'buses on them. The defendant was represented by his son.—P.C. Sankey said the offence was committed on Sunday, the 12th inst. The vehicles remained in the street two hours. He had pre- viously warned Mr. Connor about the obstruction, but no notice was token of it, ex:sept that he said .4,91Ve might as well close the yard if we cat not leave the vehicles in the street."—The Chief Constable said he was very reluctant to prosecute a respectable resident like Mr. Connor, but he was obliged to do so owine to the large number of complaints which had been received about the ob,truction in the streets named. He did not press for a heavy penalty.—Mr. Conner jun., said that whnn his father removed to Crown-street,which he would do in the course of a few months, there would be no further cause for cempleint. The day its question was an exceedingly busy one with them,as thy had no fewer than 15 funerals. They had receive] comelaints them- selves about the obstruction from the congregation of Held-street Chapel. They could not remedy the matter at present. —The Chief Constable : Once for all, Mr. Connor may take it that he will not be allowed to ob- struct the streets.—A fine of 103 and costs was imposed.

SERIOUS FARM FIRE AT BURTONWOOD,- £6OO DAMAGE.

Shortly before nine o'clock on Tuesday night. in- formation was received by telephone at the Fire Brigade Station at Warrington that a fire had broken out on the premises of Mr. Albert Sikhs w, , " I'th' Fields" Farm, Buttonwood. Superintendent Turner,the en,gineer,and a number of men promptly started for the scene of the conflagration, tsking with them the steam fire engine Major. On reach- ing the farm they found, that a dutch barn, con- taining several stacks of bay, corn, and straw, and an adjoining stack of hay were in flames. A plenti- ful supply of water was at hand and the firemen quickly got to work. Fortunately, a strong wind. wag blowing from the direction Gf the farm build- ings, otherwi4e they would have been placed in jeopardy, as the fire had got a good hold. The principal work of the firemen was directed towards savinz a couple of hay-stacks in close proximity to the barn. Only one stack however, was saved. The damage, which is covered by insurance,is estimated at between £5OO and £6OO.

WARRINGTON PERFORM POORLY AT ST. HELENS. Warrington took soaechine like a thousand sup- porters to St. Helens on Saturlay,and out of that theu- sand there were few not absolutely confluent of vt-,ory for the visitors. After the improvement manifested in the team of late, and the molerate performances by St. Helens. the expectation seemed justifiable. The result, however, told a different tale entirely. Warrington were unfortunate in having to take the fiele without the speediest of their threequarters,Harris,who was un- able to get his permit from the Northern Union. Cross filled the vacancy, Iseerwoed playing on the wing. There were about 3,000 spectators on the ground, a record gate for St. Helens this season. The weather was fine, but rather too warm for football, and the soft, heavy state of the ground did not make the game any more enjoyable from a player' poiet of view. Mr. H. Eagle: was the referee, and the teams turned out as follows : Warrington : Hallam, full back ; Isherwood. Cross, C. Wood, and Fish, thre.querters ; Bate and Ashurst, half backs; faylor, Naylor, Swift, Morrison, Fairhurst Beardman, Seholtze, and Sudlow, forwards. St. Helens : Foulkes, full back; Liversage, Barnes, Doherty. and Hunter, threeq earters ; Stott, and B evle, half backs ; Simpson, Whitely, Thompson, Carney, Dale Briers, Winstanley, and Reynolds, forwards. Taylor started the game for Warrington, who were soon on the attack and scrimmaged in the home half. Bate and Aeleurst got away, the latter passing to Wood who made a desk, only to be promptly upset. Good forward work by St. Helens compelled Warring- ton to retire to their own half, Hunter taking a pass from Boyle and gaining ground. Hallam ' was hard pressed but made a grand mark in the thick of his opponents. Stott and Briers nearly broke terough the home defence,lsherwood coming to the rescue with a clinking dribb'e to centre. Play was exceedingly, fast, both teams putting in a lot of hard work. Boyle !bunted from touch and the ball was again nearly carried over the Warrington line, Fairhurst saving pluckily. Bad kicking by Cross again let Warrington down, St. Helens pressing severely, one of the forwards crossing the War- , rington line ac toe whistle soencled. Stott made a dash for the line but was held by Hallam. Teen Warrington were penalised for informality at the scrummage, and Foulkes kicked an easy goal. Warringtoa rallied again and forced St. Helens back,. but faulty passing by Bate ma le no headway. It was now noticei that Bate was lame, and shortly after he left the game, remaining on the touch-line until the in- terval: Fairhurst took nis place behind the scrimmage. A clear punt by Morrison carriel play to the St. Helens line, but the homesters soon cleared. A flying punt sant the ball past Hallam, and an exciting race for the line ensued, the Warrington full-back winning and kicking dead at the critical moment. Warringten's play de- teriorated a lot, and the pace slackened. Boyle got away from the scrimmage and was going well for the line when Hallam upset trim. Scrambling OAF in front of the eiVar- ringtoa goal enued in Boyle picking up and drop- ping the ball jus: over the cross bar. Carney kicked off on resuming, and five minutes had not elapsed before Stott secured, and dodg- ing Wood literally walked over between the posts, and Foulkes kicked an easy goal. THE PARR HALL 6iTURDAY EVENING

CONCERTS.

The last of this series of concerts at Parr Hall was held on Saturday evening, and the hall was com- pletely filled. The artistes were :—The Merrions (Will, Harry and Fred), Madame Lottie Herod (soprano), and Mr. J. E. Warhurst (accompanist). Toe programme opened with a selection on the pianoforte by Mr. Warhurst, which was played with taste and ability and was well applauded. Miss Lottie Herod, whose rich soprano voice has bean heard on previous occasions by Warrington audiences, was heard to advantage in all her items which included "My land ot Spain:J. "The Swallows," and "Tell me my heart." In answer to the hearty applause at the conclusion of the latter song, the artist bowed her acknowledge- ment, but the audience insisted on an encore, and Miss Herod responded with the ever popular "Killarney." Ti:e Merrions had a difficult task be- fore them of entertaining the audience for the best part of the evening, but to th&r credit it must be said they accomplished the task with success, and we must not forget that all the it.ms of their ex- tensive reportoire are their own comsosition. Mr. Fred Merrion gave some capital humor- ous songs all of which were well re- ceived. The songs were entitled "The wedding day," "When he TOSO to speak," and" The Postman." In response to a recall for the latter character song he gave "The mail cart." Mr. Will Merrion also gave the parody song 66 When London sleeps "in good style. The Merrions appeared to- ge!her in some humorous trios chief of which was "The Old Boys." Thera were some fine touches of humour in this, and the Merrions took their parts of old men in a retrospective mood to perfection. They were also encored for the "Chinese trio." The conjuring _ entertainment

by Messrs. Will and Harry Merrion proved extremely funny ; and the same artistes in what was called a" musical melange" produced music from a bewildering variety of instruments. Mr. Harry Merrion's pictorial sketch" Living pictures from the music sellers" was a pleasing item. The entertainment concluded with a farce "Tim Actor" which was well given and was very laughable. Mr. J. E. Warhurst accompanied the artistes on the piano with ability.

RHONDDA GLEE SOCTETY AT PARR

IiALL

The annual concert in aid of the Warrington Dis- trict Narsing, Association was held on Thursday last week was distinguished by the appearance of the Rhondda Royal Male Choir. The hall was well filled, but there were many vacant places in the re- served seats, which is a matter to be regretted as the financial result will be somewhat affected. Last year the concert realised about £6O but it wili be somewhat less on the present occasion. The dimin- ished attendance in the reserved seats may be due to the concert being held in Lent or to what the Principal of the Training College described at the ineetiug of the Literary and Philosophical Society as "a perfect epidemic of dissipation." The pro- gramme opened with a glee "Comrades in Arms" by the choir which was under the ' conductorship of Mr. Percie G. Smith who took up the baton in the unavoidable absence of Mr. Tom Stephens, due to the illness of his daughter. "Tell ray boys," he telegraphed, "to keep their spirits up," an injunction which was faithfully carried out. During the first chorus Mr. Smith was occupied with the complex task of getting a firm grip of his material. But it was not long before the choir found itself on solid ground, and by the time the second piece "The Summer" was reached, the swing and balance of the parts were all that could be desired. Complying with a recall the ever popular chorus " The March of the Men of Harlech " was given, and pleased the ' audience to such an extent that a double encore was demanded. "The Soldier's Fare- well " was then given. As a descriptive piece nothing could be finer than the choir's inter- pretation of "The Tyrol," into which the singers entered with spirit and energy. Still another en- core was demanded, and the choir responded with "Annabelle Lee." Perhaps the most adea mit* sung ' chorus of the evening was De Rules" Destruction ~ or Gaza "in which the light and shade were fully ' marked. Other choruses given were" Hues of Day," the hunting chorus "Night and Day," and" Land of our Fathers." At the close "God save the Queen" was sung, Mr. David Davies singing the solo part in Welsh. Altogether the choir gave a highly cre- ditable performance, and justified the reputation it has gained. To turn to the leading artistes of the evening the palm certainly falls to Miss A. Back- sheen Wood, a young and accomplished solo violin- ist. The graceful " Bolero " by Sarasate received careful and delicate treatment. In res- ponse to the repeated applause she was constrained to bow her acknowledgments, but there were cries for "more" and she complied with " The Austrian Hymn" by Leonaed. In the second half the same artiste played "Andante" and"Mazurka"by Virienaw- ski. Both were brilliantly executed and a second encore was conceded. Miss Wood created a most favourable impre:ssion, and she seems destined to rank amongst the virtuosi of the day. Miss Eleanor Jones, R.C.M. (soprano) secured an encore for her first song "Mia Piccerella." and in response sang with piquancy and effect "Coming thro' the Rye." Two other solos "Vainka's Song"and "Tears' duet " Martial Spirit." Mr. James' two songs were "Largo al Factatum" and "When bright eyes glance," in both of which he displayed a robust and cultivated voice. He was deservedly encored and sang "The Yeoman's Wedding" which further pleased the audience. The pennillions were a feature of the concert. The vocalist was a gentleman who hides his identity under the pseudonym of 13es Dar, and his contributions were

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDEN Fs. Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor. Matter intended for publication must be written on one side only of the paper. Com>spondents are strongly advised to writs briefly. A short letter has much greater chance of insertion than a long one.

WARRINGTON CORPORATION BILL. Sir,—The Mayor having entered the arena his circular letter now in the lands of the ratepayers invites respect- ful and independent criticism. In the third paragraph of his circular, writing of the water strpply for domesic purposes, the Mayor says, "The daily quantity pumped (at Winwick) at the pre- sent time is 1,400,000 gallons." Now if the number of consumers is fairly estimated at 63.000 it follows that we have a daily supply of more than 22 gallons for every unit of the popul,, tier]. Or, to put it in another form, we have at least 110 gallons of water per diem for every family offiye persons in the Borough. To me at least this seems a liberal provision and one that, with proper supervision and economy, might meet all needful requirements for some years to come. In some places with which I am acquainted, when a falling off of water occurs through long continued dry weather or other causes, the authorities do not rush into new expendi- ture, but caution the people to be as economical as possible in their use of water until the normal condition of the supply has been restored. In the fourth paragraph of his letter the Mayor writes, "It has been stated that the Corporation are in a posi- tion to spend at least £50,000 in the construction of new works without having recourselo the rates and this I statement I confirm. The increisiug revenue of the waterworks ler dertaking will in addition to this sum enable the Corporation to spend not less than £lO,OOO a year on the prop red works without having to touch the rates." I must confess at once that, to my mind, there is some obscurity in these sentences. Am I to under- stand the Mayor to mean that there is £50,000 now standing o the creqlt of the ware= concern as accumu- lated pr,ofits available for Corporati,n purooe• s I must answer this question in the negative 3nd the Mayor him- self is my aurhority for doing so. The wat-rworks were acquired in 1890, aril sneaking of the reault of the b4r- gain in the conebviing linia of his scr,r,th parattraph,the Mayor sass, " The purchase of the materworks has proved a great boon and instead el a ~a•Te upon the rates serf;ral thousand pour d haee be-n e ,reed." That is to say, thenet earnings of the water con.ytn for the past eight or nine veers have a-roonted "to several thousands of pounds." But, for some reason or other . which I cannot understand, we are left in the dark as to the number of these thousands. Assuming then, that the £50,000 only exists in the minds of the promoters of the Bill and will have to be borrowed when required, such borrowing will involve an expenditure of at least £1,250 a year for interest so long as the money remains unpaid. Are the annual pro- fits of the water department such as to bear this charge and the additional £lO,OOO which the Mayor informs us the Corporation will be able to spend" on the proposed works without having to touch the rates?" Or, in other words. are the duly audited profits of the concern £11,250 a year? This is a vital question and a properly authenticated answer to it should determiae the accept- ance or rejection of the Bil. Yours truly, lONA,

Sir,—l must ask the courtesy of your affording mo stillmore space in your paper to reply to the Mayor's letter, appearing in your 148 t issue. In the first place, lam glad t see that the Mayor does not attempt to answer, and, therefore must ba taken not to sericu3lv dispute, three out of the four points I originally under- took to prove. Thege three points now virtually admitted are : __ . .

. _ (1.1 That the supply of water at Bold is in every resvect as permanent as the supply at Win- wick. (2) Tint it is in quantity approximately three times what the rlorporation hope to get by their expensive Winwick scheme. (3) That it can bs supplied to Warriegton at less than one half tho cost of obtaining the Win. wick water. _ _ _ _

The only remaining point, and one which is very properly raised by the Mayor, is as to the quality of the Bold water compared with that of Winwick ; and upon this point allow me to thank him for his fairness in at once admitting that the two waters are equally pure and wholesome—the only question being with reference to their relative hardness. The Mayor publishes extracts from four analyses, the first of which is an analysis made by Mr. J. C. Bell of the Winwiek water, whereby he returns the total hard- ness at 11.75. This he compares with the secend analysis made by Mr. Ruddock of the Bold water, whieh he states has a total hardness of 30. If these analyses are to be depended upon, the Mayor has made a very good case, but I say at once that they are obviously incorrect. I have thought that there is but one way to bring conviction to the minds of the Corporation. I, therefore submitted most carefully sealed samples of the Bold and Winwick water to Mes3rs. Grace. Calvert. and Thomsen, of Manchester, the mo:t eminent analysts in the kingdom. I marked the sample of Winwick water N. Land the Bold w +ter No. 2 Their analysis I received only this morning, and I append a copy of it to this letter. Your readers will see that upon the question of purity both waters are certified to be of high-class, tAe Bold water, however, containing somewhat less organic matter than the Winw;ck water. On the question of hardness the analysis shows :

Winviek. Temporary hardness 11'73 ... 18 3i Permanent „ 506 ... 0.91

Totsl hardness The above, I think, is abiolut proof that with refer- ence to hardnets the Bold water and the Winwick water are, as' have said before, identical. I would go even further than the Mayor in admitting that it mistht be wise for the Corporation to uneertake some softening scheme for both waters, and upon this point I have made certain inquiries, and find that at S. Helens a softening process is in use which appears to act well. The quantitirs treated are upwards of 730,000,000 gallons pr annum, and the average harineas of the water blfore treatmln, i; 18.4 ; after treatment 11•1. The cest of qr. treatment is analysed by the w ..t3r engineer as follow; : _ Cost per 1,003 gallons.

Pence. Labour 0123 Lime 0.115 Filter Cinth Fuel and repair a

0 432 Sinking fund and interest

Total per 1,000 gallons

This goes to show that at a cost of but little over id. per thousand gallons the hardness of the Winwick water could he reduced by very many degrees, and I believe that this irould meet with the approval of all water consumers in the town. To conciude, it is now virtually admitted that War- rington can obtain their water frpm Bold it' larger qnantitirs than from Winwicv with equal permanency ard at less than half the cost. I submit that I have also proved that the two waters are identical in quality. If there is still any doubt about this last point let certifiel samples be submitted by the Corporatioa themselves, to any eminent analyst. and should such analyses show anything like the difference in hardness between the two waters which the Mayor alleges, I would at once undertake n-,t only to Vita but to speak on behalf of the Winwick scheme. If, on the other hand, the: two waters are identlcal in quality, as I firmly believe them to be, then I tlynk the in'iabitants of Warriogton are entitled to ask he C-wporation t 3 reconsiler their deci- sion' and to spare the ratepayers the heavy expense conterop'ated by the (chew as at present suggesto.l: Yours &c.,

HENRY FAIRCLOUGH, Chairman of the Bold Hall &tales Limited.

WARRINGTON AND A WATER FAMINE. Sir,—l don't wish unnecessarily to alarm my fellow- townsmen, but when I am told by the Water Engineer that again within the past few days tho water in the well at Winwick has fallen as low as 19 ffet, it is quite rime to acquaint the town with the serious position which confronts us. It is quite on the cars that we may have to cut off or curtail the Winwick supply for trading purposes within the next twelve months in order to husband our resources for domestic use only,unless we are empower- ed.ss I trust we shall be. to go on with our Bill.To cur- tail the supply of water for trade purposes means restric- tion of employment, loss of wages, inability to pay rents and shopkeepers' bills, and what is even more serious, the probability ot permanently injuring the growth and prosperity of the whole community. I appeal to my fellow rateyayers to seriougy consider the vital import- ane.l of these facts, and lam certain they will then support the Bill.—Yours, etc:,

H. ROBERTS.
