OUR LOCAL-CaIIRCHES.

THE CHRTSTADELPFIIAN MEETING ROOM. Mr. T. S. McNair, of Warrington, lectured in the Christade'phian Room on Sunday, on" A much used and much abused passage of Scripture." i.e John 3 and 16," God so loved the world that he gave his only begotten Son, that whosoever believeth in him should not perish, but have everlasting life." The theme of this incomes they would observe was God's love, and not the implacab'e hate which oldfashinned theologians repreecn- ted. Christ's mission showed the tenderness of God to limners beings, and it was not a matter of God being re- conciled to men, but men to God. The object of this love and reconciliation was that they might not perish. The word p^rish was not what theologians would lead them to believe—a living in torment for ever—it meant to pass away completely. Of several instances and illuctrationi of the correct Biblical meaning of the word, the 37th Psalm and 201 i verse was most emphatic. "The wicked shall perish, and the enemies of the Lord shall be as the fat of lambs, they, shall consume; into smoke shall i hey consume away." Some were like the beasts that perish, and this perishing condition was the remit of sin. "The wa.es of sin is death but the gift of Ge,d is eternal " Jews Christ's words were" I give ueto them eternal life an-I they shall never perish." God off re eternal life. Those who desired the gift could have it by believing. Belief meant a hearty acceptance of Him and His teaching be'h moral and doctrinal. A. mere verbal expression of faith rould not rave, for faith without works is dead, being Meng. If they believed and were haptizod, they would not perish, but would have ever- resting life. A believer,vras one who realized who Christ Was and his relationship to God and to Abraham, that he was he Messiah and that he was to set up the King- dem of God on the earth. Baptism was the outward and visible sign of union with Christ, and had been abandoned becauSe the belief was not in the churches. If they had been baptized and were walkieg worthily it would be well with them, hut if not what then. The invitation was wheseeeer. They might enclude them- selves by net complying with the conditions. for Silva- tion was a great gift and not to be trifled with. In the absence of Christ they would soon all pass away and he earnestly urged them to accept salvation while it was yet" Today,"

ITHE CHURCH AND SOCIAL QUESTIONS. At St. James' Church, on Senriey afternoon the Rev. F E. pawll took for his suhjec; "The, Church and Social Qusstions ; Christ's message to the Industrial World." The spraker c ompared the solar system to our life, and said the great controlling influence of our life was He who was to us what the sun was to the solar system. There were some conditions of life we could not help but there ware certaie relationships we made for ourselves. For instance an Englishman could not help being barn an Englishman. but it was the second-' are. relationships that const;tuted the real forces in human life. Mr. Powell said in human society there were three princip'ee very analogous to three in the solar system. First there was the individuality, the personal force of each human being. Secondly,there was a great Sovereign Power which limited the conditions of the in- dividuality of each human being. And thirdly,besides the primary relationship of each human being to his God there were secondary relationsbipe—some made for them, e g , the family, the national; some they made for themselves, e g., neighourhood, clubs, societies, Sec. The human mind was so constituted that is was apt to become one-siiel in its view of life, Some emphasized' the godly relationship to the exclusion Of the others Others, on the other hand, forgot that primary relation ship altogether. The anond gr-at aspect of life wa individdalism. That force had built up their civilization on its material File, and was therefore not altogether had, but a true force in life. But its unrestricted play had brough . untoe:l nokery and desoeation. With the increase of wealth there had been a simultaneous increase in want. Their industrial system was mainly based upon undiluted, unrestrained indi viduelism, and was worthy rA the condemnation of all tight-thinking people. 'A system which for the sake of greedy gain encouraged unhealthy and dangerous trades like the match fac celes,anct white had poisoning, which permits women working in insanitary ceinditione, to earn is per day of 17 hours; which bail 4i millions of the population ever en the verge of pauperism, such a system csuld not be too severely re- probated. The third aspect of life was socfalistic. Here not selfish but brotherly instincts were appealei to. The salvation of the race was not less important than the salvation of the individual. And that they were on the eve ot vast changes was =tam. So far from fearing; these they welcomed them with a thankful heart: Lot them try not to be one-sided. Let them recognize the three principles of human society which Christians recognized: personal self-love, socialistic fraternity and the fear of God. The individual ceuld be perfected only when he co-operated with society. Society could only be perfected when the individual inanhoxl and womanhood of each was developed and metered. And both Individualism and Socialism, to be of use and a b'eseing, must be har- monized and balanced by the love of God. ' THE REV. W. H. DRUMMOND AND THE PEACE ' CRUSADE.

The Rev. W. H. Drammeod delivered a sermon in Cairo-street Chapel on Sunday evening on "The Peace CruFade," taking for his text the vrarda " On earth peace." He remarked that they as Christian people would not only have the opportunity as Cit'Zlll4 cine community of exoreesiag their le eine. with regard to the great crusade of peace which was going on in the country, but they wished that night more openly and avowedly to associate it with their religion. The preacher ailuded to the objntions raised to the Czar's mattifesto, and stid they might plead for the cause of peace on purely economic grounds, as they re- membered the terrible drain upon natonal resources, especially in some of the poorer couetries of the Contin- ent, GII social and political grounds as they saw the grave danger of the growth of the military spirit,as they watched with increasing anxiety the struggle that was at present going on in France, where the military spirit was corrupting the national sense of ju,tice and doing much to rob them of that respect they felt for a country which had done such great things in the past for literature, art, and fr the greater interests of men. That nieht let them rather lay strong emphasis on the Christian motives,for though the economic and political reasoes might have very great weight in their minds, and 'justly so, they struck a stronger and deeper chord when they appealed directly te the great Christian Peals of human life—to the great sacredness of human life. No longer were they content te say that a man most live and struggle for himgelf alone. They were members one of another, not only when they were reading the New Testament, but also when they were engaged in politics or in industrial enterpOsseand that sams motive tnight work a profound charge in their international relations. The more the Christian motive of fellowship animated them the more they would be prepared to dedicate themselves to every movement which made for the widening of brotherhood amongst men. This was a movement of the peeple,end it depended for its 81.13CEIS upon the people. It was a religious movement; he did net mean a clerical move- ment or that it had any religious class feeling about it, that it was simply a matter to be discussed in pulpits, but a religieus movement bee toss it directly appealed to the deepest interests and to the meet anselt.sh desires of the people as a whelp.

PIONEER LECTURES AT EARLESTOWN. THE GROWTH OF THE BRITISH

The firs'. lecture in the series on " The Growth cf tha British Empire" was gives in the Town Hall, Eirlestown on Wedneslay evening, by Mr. H. Yule Oldham, M.A., reader in Geography in the University of Cambridge, who took for his subjelt "The Fiel I of Empire." TOot9 was again a large aud.ence. Mr.R. Birton presided and said he thought that night was the beginning of their lectures. That which was given by Dr. Da Linger was, _ _ .

if he might term it, a tit-bi%-and that night they ban the lectures on the British &nein, and Ell thought they would agree with him that the Committe3 had acted wisely in bringing such a subject before them this whi- ter. (Hear, hear.) The I.acturer said they would have noticed, doubt- _ . .

lessly, ita the little syllabus which had been issuei, that in the forefront of that page devoted to his lecture was something said concerning position,and though it might be tainiliar to most of them, he would drlw their at- tention to the fact that position induced importtnt re-

sults. If they wore setting out to establish any great centre of business, he thought, undoubtedly, they would select a positioa that was central, bat supposing they had a choice between a warehouse or shop on the outskirts of the town, or a business in the centre, there was no doubt they would chose the centre. Poiltion was of the most vital importance. There was a school in the Midlands which owed its

popularity to its position, he meant a great school at Rughy.The lights in the hall were then turned down,and by means of the oxy-hvdrogen light, he showed many illustrations of maps, &a, the first being descriptive of England in relation to early ceutres of civilisation. Toe first explorers were the Phoenicians, who in voyaging through Europe formed Colonies round its shores, and pushing oit into the Atlantic reached the British Isles probably, and possibly may have sailed all round Africa, but they covered up their traces eff,ctually. Their wonderful achinv2-ments became practically forgotten,and their work had to be done again by that extraordinary

race the Greeks, and the pillars of Hercules were set up, which they would recognise as the two great points guarding the Mediterranan. In course of time the British Isles were opened up by the Greeks, setl3l three centuries brfore the Christian era. At this time the Greeks had no idea whatever as to the globular shape of the earth. Erroneous ideas as to the size of the earth cropped up, and the dimensions given had Co be reduced and in the middle of the second century the world had been reduced in imagination to two thirds of its actual size. A map of the world according to Ptolemy. A.D., 150 was then thrown on the screen. In this map Asia was made te stretch so far te the east that it stretched more than half way round the earth, and the British Isles were'shown in the other coreer of the in of the world hopelessly shut out in an oh ;cure cornsr. After this followed centuries of the deepest darkness, such that he could not attempt to discribe to them. He junaird from the second to the sixth century,a,ed he showed what was the best map of the world that had ever been produced, drawn by, a Monk named Cosms Indicopleustis, who represented the world as oblong. Bat in the next cen- tury the world was shown circular, some'hirg like a flat plate, and diyided into three parts, Errepe, Asia and Africa' and the ocean flewing all round, this was drawn by Imlago Munadin. Another map of' the world was shown the great idea of which was that Jerusalem was in the The beauty of such a map was that a traveller was able to reach the edge of the earth, and peer at the heavens beyond. Venice in the middle ages had 'Meg in it a certain family called Palo, and one or two sons, Maros Polo by name became in the 13th century a great traveller, and left his native city able the middle of the 13th century and wended his way along the whole length of Asia until he reached the furthest extremes and saw the great cities of China. He made his way to Pales- tine through Persia, Turkestan to Pekin. When Marco Polo arrived at Pekin the great Mongolian empire had split up. He found in China numerous signs of civilisa- tion. Hang Chau was described as a great city con- taining over a million houses. It had admirable orgeni- Rations, with police and fire brigades. When the fire gong was sonnded no one was allowed to go out into the streets except the fire brigade. and this he sail was "a principal they might very well adopt in England. The streets were well paved and well drained ; they had innumerable canals, and there were 1,200 brideee. Marie Polo while there heard of a wonderful island whinti lay out in the sea, which was described as a wonderfully rich island, where there were precious stones and pearls, and a palace, the interior of which was practically of gold. This was Japan. After returning to Venice, Mareo Polo was captured in a great fight and carried off to Genoa where he was confined in a prison. During his confinement he committed to paper the story of his wanderings, and that story proved an incentive to Western Nations to find new routes to the east, A modern map was then exhibited and the lecturer said it showed almost exactly what was shown in the 14th century. The richest commerce in the world was the trade with India, Silks frore India, spices frsm China, made their way to Venice, where they were distribetel over the rest of Europe and that seas what made Venice rich. He then went on to explain the finding of a new route to the east. Prince Henry son of King John of Portugal spent his whole life in sending expeditioa after expedition in order to penetrate into the unke, we in order to prove if possible that an ocean really ley all round Africa to the East. Before he began his work, no man knew anything beyond Morocco opposite the Canary Islands. But he sailed on as far as Gambia, which was the limit in his life. He had spent 45 years in exploring and unveiling the coast of Africa and after his death his spirit lived on, and his succeesers explored the coast bit by bit. Fifteen years after his death, the equator was passed and gradually thee crept on always on towards the South, until at trait some 25 years after Prince Henry's death, one bolder than the rest did a very brilliant feat. Bartholomew Dyes went on to the orange state and sailed on to Algoa bay, and after sailing some time along the coast he found it went towards the North east. He interned to Portugal and reported his result stating he had found a stormy cape. The end of Africa had Iren found, and the news being of a joyful character the stormy cape was named the Cape of Good Hope. Some eleven years later Prince Henry's seherne was improved on. Fran- cesea de Game sailed from Portugal and after reaching Mombasa he struck across the ocean to India, and from there he passed on to the SOiC9 islands. Whilst Africa was b bag unveiled, events of an important nature were transpiring elsewhere. It was in Genoa that the great eanlorer Christopher Columbus was born. At the age of 14 he went to sea to learn the business of a sailor. The lecturer then went on to describe the carrying out of the scheme formulated by Tuscanelli a Florentian, by which hell said if Columbus would'sail to'the West he wonld besure to come to the Eset. It was in werkine out this idea that Columbus fell in with the island which afterwards became known as America. Columbus had 'been sailing to the West, and had only been on the sea 8 weeks when he came to the land, and he naturally thought he had reached Ihdiat He -returned to Spain end gave a wonderful account of what he had done. It was a vast error, how- ever, which was perpetuated to this day in the name West Indies. The name America Came from a famous Flerentian, Amerige Yelper-mei. This man followed in the wake of Colninhus and explored S -uth America.and from the animals Sze., that he flaw came to the emelt]. sien that this was an unknown land, and that he had discevercd a new world. The re etries that Columbus had discovered were still called t 1,7 trie names he gave them, and were ennneend te he off the coast of Asia, bet the name of America only applied to the South. Gradu- ally it crept op co the North, Ind finally the whole country brceme known as America. The discovering of the Straits of Magellan by Ferdinand Magellan was next referred to, this voyager having fur the first time cir- cemnavisrated the world. Pneraite were shown of John Haeokyns, Sir Francis Drake, Sir Walter Raleigh, the latter he said being in the Ezebethen period the instigater of exploration', and the founder to a great extent of our Colonial Empire. 500 years ago Venice was the centre of the world. At that time Eurole only looked to the East, to the West was the pathless barrier of the Atlantic. After the discovery of the Cape route, which diverted for 31 cen- turies she stream of Eastern commei•ce, from its old hi,- tonic route through the Mediterranean, Venice, decayed, and Lisbon became the commercial centre, but neither were now the great centres of activity. What might be called the commercial centre of the world is London out- else the Bank of England. Venice and Lisboa had had their day and from the banks of the Thames and from the Houses of Parliament was stretched out a controlling hand over the greatest empire of the world, and that we had won this great empire was due above all te the enormous change' wrought by the heroic eff ,rts of those great exnlorers who had unveiled for us the fiedd of em- pire. (Applause.) Votes of thank, to he Lecturer and Chairman termin- ated the proceedings.

The chief feature of the report of the Public Benefit Boot Comranv. Ltd., is the same as was acted a rad ago, namely, the large am mot of unemployed capita pesqessed by the company. The cash in hand and at bankers amounts to no less than £34,793, while the re- serve fund of £B,OBO is invested in debentures of other compinies. Taken together, there is at the disposition of the directors nearly £43,000 which they have not sern fit to invest by opening out further branch shops. We do not war, this in reproach, flr we entirely endorse the steady-going' po'icy which the Board has evidently adopted. Notwithstanding their large idle capital, the Public Benefit Co. can still pay 11. per cent upon its ordinary shares. Snre!y it is totter to do this than to engage in reckless competition all over the country,with convquences which cinnot be ago.; tained until too late for remedy. The report cle2r'y endorses our own im- pression that the multiple shop business has reached the print where further expansion is dangerous.

NOTES BY THE WAY AT EAALESTOWN

(BY A CORRF.SPONDENT.) FOOTBALL.

Contrary to expectation, Haydock were un- able to vanquish Earlestown on Saturday last, in their Alliance fixture. A pod number of suppor- ters accompanied them, and they were nwarded by a splendid exhibition of football. Earlestown in- cluded Harrisson, a new acquaintance, in the team, but owing to Leach having failed to put in an ap- pearance, Lee had to join the forward rank, and Alldridge was put half-hack. The result showed that the absence of Leach was a most fortunate occurrence, as Aildridge played a determined game all through. and was generally losst of the half- back line. He was instructed to keep close eye on Smith, one of the liaydock wing players, and he carried out his inktructions to the letter, clinging to Smith like "an old man of the sea." Haydock played their" full Lancashire League team," but it availed them nothing against the Alli- ance team of Earlestown, ani in fact they had a very narrow shave of being defeated, as Earlestown pressed the greater part of the second half of the game. There WaS much dissatisfaction expressed at the close of the game, owing to the referee having it was said, called time, two minutes before the 80 minutes were up. Whether this made any differ- ence in the game or not, lam not prepared to Fay, but Earlestown vi,,,re clustering round the Haydock goal at the time.

The protest which Earlestown lodged against Bamfurlong in connection with the tie in the com- petition for the Wigan cup in consequence of the ground not beinq the regulation length of 100 yards, was considered on I.londay evening. After hearing the evidence for and against the protest, the committee decided that E irlestown were justi- fied in their action,and allowed the protest,ordering the tie to be replayed at Earlestown to-morrow (Saturday.) Much feeling has been exhibited in all the games hitherto, but in order to prevent any un- fairness, it has been decided that Cie linesmen shall Le neutral.

On last week's form Earlestown should win, and I think, seeing they will b,l on their own ground, they should easily dispose of t!,:e Colliery men.

Enilestown Rovers met W arringt m St. Elphin's at Etirlestown on Saturday, in the Warrington and District League, and the Rovers; succeeded in de- feating St. Elphin's by 4 goals to 2.
